#10859. The Privacy Risks of Browser Extensions: What Developers Should Know

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The extension a developer installed three years ago can probably read everything they do in the browser today.

General online security advice from VPNpro and similar guides cover the network side of staying safe, but they cannot see what an extension does inside the browser. That layer is more the developer's responsibility. The risk is complicated, but the fix is simple: request fewer permissions, narrower host access, and less data, and review every update. Here is a walkthrough of each.

Think about what a developer has open on a normal day: a cloud console, a staging dashboard, a private repo, maybe webmail. One over-permissioned extension sees all of it.

The most common mistake is asking for broad access early and planning to trim it later. Later rarely arrives.

A manifest like this should raise eyebrows:

A developer who cannot justify a permission in a short sentence likely shouldn't include the permission in the build.

Host permissions deserve their own attention because they define how far the extension can reach. A few habits make a real difference:

This also helps adoption. People hesitate when an extension asks to read every site they visit. A narrow request looks, and is, more trustworthy.

Analytics and error logging are where good intentions leak. A crash report that includes the full page URL may contain a session token in the query string. A “usage event” may capture page titles from internal tools.

The most secure approach is to collect only the data a feature needs and to process the data locally if possible. When data has to be sent, it should be stripped down to the absolute minimum. Avoid sending query strings, and truncate URLs to the domain name where possible. Never send page content if it is not absolutely necessary.

Remote code is another trap. Manifest V3 restricts remotely hosted code for a good reason. A script fetched at runtime can modify its behavior after the fact, and one can’t audit what it will do in the future. Bundle everything and the code that is reviewed will be identical to the code that is run.

A good extension today can become a risky one next year. Two patterns keep repeating.

Ownership changes. Popular extensions get sold, and the new owner ships an update with tracking or worse. The Great Suspender, a widely used tab tool, ended up flagged as malware after a change of hands.

Compromised publisher accounts. If an attacker phishes the developer account, they can push a malicious update to every existing user. Cyberhaven’s extension was hit by a phished administrator account in late December 2024.

The defenses are mostly boring, which is a good sign. Hardware-key two-factor authentication on the publisher account, a short list of people allowed to publish, and a review step before each release stops most of it. Lockfiles, pinned versions and regular dependency audits cover the code side.

Teams that only install extensions can apply the same thinking. Keep an approved list, review updates that change permissions, and remove anything nobody uses. Following security news is worthwhile too, since incidents involving popular extensions tend to be reported publicly.

It is only one layer, though. A VPN protects traffic in transit. It does nothing about an add-on that reads page content inside the browser, so it never replaces a proper permission review.

Simpler habits do more here. A separate browser profile for development keeps test extensions away from personal accounts. Fake or low-privilege accounts for testing avoid exposing real credentials. And installers for any tool, VPN clients included, should come from the official site or store, never from a mirror.

Safe extensions come down to restraint. Ask for less access, collect less data, send less off the device, and explain more to the user. Developers who treat every permission as a liability end up with extensions people can trust, and teams that review what they install avoid the worst surprises.

#10854. Costco’s $1.50 hot dogs being resold for $18 on black market delivery service

Costco’s iconic food court items are being sold on new delivery apps for a massive markup in price, with the iconic $1.50 hot dog and drink costing $18. Seriously.

For a lot of Costco customers, a trip to the warehouse isn’t complete without picking up something from the food court. Some shoppers grab a hot dog and drink before they do any shopping, while others wait to grab a treat before they exit.

The iconic hot dog and drink combo still costs $1.50 in the US, with Costco CEO Ron Vachris promising to never raise it.

However, if you’re in Southern California and can’t just dash to your nearest warehouse, there is a new food delivery startup that you can order from. Just don’t expect the cheap food items to come, well, cheap.

As per SFGate, a new delivery service called NoTip is actually delivering Costco’s food court items to customers. Costco is available on Uber Eats, but you aren’t able to order any of the hot food or sweet treats.

That’s where NoTip comes in, but they do charge a big markup. Speaking to SFGate, NoTip’s Jack Chen noted that the $1.50 hot dog and drink combo actually costs $18 on the app.

“A lot of offices are ordering from me. It’s the company’s money. As long as you’re not too expensive, I mean, they’ve got the money to blow,” Chen told SFGate. “You’d be surprised how some people are just craving it specifically.”

Costco releases brand-new food court dessert and it’s the “best” yet

Costco’s most popular items actually aren’t available on Uber Eats or DoorDash

Costco CEO promises he’ll never raise price of $1.50 hot dog after joining taste test trend

The NoTip founder argues that “it only adds more money” to Costco, seeing as his service is pretty busy. “I love Costco, and I would like to get more business to Costco.”

It isn’t the first time that customers have ended up paying a premium for items that aren’t on delivery services.

Some drivers have been charging $50 for burger runs to In-N-Out because the chain has rejected services like Uber Eats and DoorDash.

#10851. Man fined hundreds of dollars after giving vape to quokka for photo

A man has been fined after a photo showing a vape being held to a quokka’s mouth on an Australian island sparked a police investigation.

The image, which, according to police, was taken on Rottnest Island off the coast of Perth on November 2, circulated on social media and prompted authorities to investigate the incident.

According to 7news, Western Australia Police traced the image to a 29-year-old man, who was subsequently issued a $200 (approximately $130 USD) infringement notice.

“Investigators tracked the image to a 29-year-old man, who was subsequently issued with a Rottnest Island Authority Infringement by police,” authorities said.

The photo appears to show an electronic cigarette being placed against the animal’s mouth, although it remains unclear whether the quokka inhaled any vapor or was simply biting the device.

Quokkas are native to Western Australia, and touching or feeding the animals is prohibited. Visitors who interfere with quokkas can receive an on-the-spot fine of $200, while prosecutions under biodiversity conservation laws can carry penalties of up to $10,000.

Western Australia Premier Roger Cook condemned the image, describing it as “disgusting.”

“Our quokkas are international drawcards for Western Australia and Rottnest Island because of their warm, welcoming nature,” he said.

“Any attempt to put quokkas at risk or harm them should be met with consequences.”

#10848. Browser Fingerprinting Explained: How Websites Identify You and How Developers Can Reduce It

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most articles on this topic stop at the scary part. This one is written for people who build websites, so we’ll go a bit further: what goes into a fingerprint, what the collection code actually looks like, and what you can change in your own projects.

Think of a fingerprint as a stack of clues rather than a single value. Your time zone is shared by millions of people. So is your screen resolution. Put twenty clues like that together, though, and the crowd shrinks fast.

Here’s roughly where the clues come from.

HTTP headers. Before any JavaScript runs, every request already tells the server a lot. The User-Agent string and the Accept-Language header give away your browser, its version, your OS, and your languages. Chromium browsers also send Client Hints.

JavaScript APIs. Plenty of everyday APIs leak device details. screen.width and screen.height, navigator.hardwareConcurrency (your CPU core count), navigator.deviceMemory (Chromium only), navigator.platform, and your time zone all add something. Plugin lists used to be a goldmine, but modern browsers now return a fixed, generic list, so that one’s mostly dead.

Rendering. This is the clever bit. Ask two computers to draw the exact same text, and the pixels won’t match perfectly. Fonts, graphics drivers, and the GPU itself all nudge the output slightly. Same browser, same version, different hardware, different result.

Network details. Your IP address, connection type, and even the way your browser sets up a TLS connection can help. IPs change, so these clues are less reliable. Trackers mostly use them to back up a match they already suspect.

How unique does this make you? Back in 2010, the EFF’s Panopticlick study tested around 470,000 browsers and found that about 84% of them had a unique fingerprint. (The project still exists, now called Cover Your Tracks.) Later studies with bigger samples found lower numbers, especially on phones, since a lot of phones share near-identical hardware. The percentages move around, but the basic idea hasn’t changed.

Canvas fingerprinting gets talked about the most. Here’s a basic version:

Read through it and you’d never guess it was tracking code. It draws an orange box, writes some text, and exports the image. The catch is that anti-aliasing and subpixel rendering vary from device to device, so that exported string varies too.

The data URL is long and clunky, so trackers usually hash it into something shorter. With the Web Crypto API, that’s a few extra lines:

WebGL fingerprinting follows the same idea with 3D graphics. A script draws a hidden scene and also reads the WEBGL_debug_renderer_info extension, which can reveal your GPU’s make and model. Audio fingerprinting is sneakier. The script generates a sound, runs it through an AudioContext, and hashes the result. Different audio stacks produce slightly different numbers.

It’s worth remembering that the Canvas API, WebGL, and the Web Audio API were built for charts, games, and music apps. Tracking is a side effect of how much they expose. The W3C’s guidance on mitigating fingerprinting in web specifications covers how standards authors try to avoid adding new signals.

You might not write a single line of fingerprinting code yourself and still end up involved. There are three common ways that happens.

Your third-party scripts can do it without asking you. Analytics tags, ad scripts, chat widgets, social buttons. Each of them can call the same APIs your own code can. If your page loads fifteen of them, that’s fifteen possible fingerprinters. Checking what each script does has quietly become part of the job.

Browsers are closing the door. Chrome froze most of the User-Agent string and moved detailed info into Client Hints. Safari has Advanced Fingerprinting Protection. Firefox blocks known fingerprinting scripts, and Brave randomizes values returned by fingerprintable APIs. If your site depends on the old, wide-open APIs, you’re building on ground that’s shrinking every release.

Most of this comes down to one rule: collect less, and know why you’re collecting what’s left.

Search your codebase for the high-entropy stuff: canvas exports, WebGL renderer lookups, font detection, and calls to navigator.userAgentData.getHighEntropyValues(). If a feature still works without them, take them out.

In many teams, the biggest win is boring. Someone added a fingerprinting library years ago for one fraud check, nobody reviewed it since, and it’s still loading on every page. Deleting it helps more than any clever fix.

The classic User-Agent string packs browser, exact version, OS, and CPU architecture into one header. Client Hints break that apart so you only get what you ask for.

By default, Chromium browsers already send three basic hints: Sec-CH-UA, Sec-CH-UA-Mobile, and Sec-CH-UA-Platform. For most sites, that’s plenty, and you don’t need to request anything extra. If a feature really does need more detail, ask for that one hint with Accept-CH:

Skip full version numbers, architecture, and device model unless you have a real reason. Each extra hint makes your visitors a little easier to tell apart.

Keep in mind that only Chromium-based browsers support User-Agent Client Hints. Firefox and Safari won’t send them, so you’ll need a fallback. Where you can, feature detection beats browser detection anyway.

Here’s an uncomfortable truth: once a script runs on your page, it can call canvas, WebGL, and audio APIs, and there’s no header that switches those off. What you can control is which scripts run and how close they get to your page.

None of these is a silver bullet. Together, they cut down how much code gets the chance to fingerprint your users at all.

If your app draws on canvas (dashboards, editors, browser games), that output can be fingerprinted too. Try not to hand raw canvas data to third-party scripts.

Fonts count as well. Load fifteen unusual web fonts and you’ve given every script on the page an easy font fingerprint. A system font stack blends in better, and for interface text, most users won’t notice the difference.

Sometimes you’ll want to keep a fingerprint, for example to flag logins from a device you haven’t seen before. That’s fine, as long as you treat it with care:

Before you ship, open your site in Firefox with Enhanced Tracking Protection set to Strict, and in Brave with fingerprinting protection turned on. If something breaks, it was relying on a signal browsers are trying to get rid of.

Fix the feature. Don’t ask users to turn their protection down. Browsers are only going to get stricter from here.

What works better is a browser with fingerprinting protection built in, such as Brave, Firefox on strict settings, Safari, or Tor Browser. These either add noise to the signals or make everyone look the same. Extensions that block canvas and font reads can help too. So can simply updating your browser, since each release tends to remove a few more leaky details.

No single tool will stop fingerprinting completely. The good news is that it doesn’t have to. Fingerprinting works on probabilities, so making the signals noisier is often enough to break the match.

#10846. UK considering banning under 16s from eBay to “keep children safe”

The UK government is considering banning children under 16 from using eBay, Vinted, and other online marketplaces as part of its growing crackdown on platforms that allow young users to interact with strangers.

Britain is already preparing to ban under-16s from major social media platforms, including Instagram, TikTok, and Snapchat, with restrictions expected to take effect in spring 2027.

However, the government could go even further, potentially preventing children from using popular shopping websites over concerns about online grooming.

According to Sky News, Culture Secretary Lisa Nandy confirmed that officials are considering expanding the ban to online marketplaces.

Asked during an appearance on the BBC’s Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg whether eBay and Vinted could be included, Nandy responded: “We are looking seriously at doing so in order to keep children safe.”

The Sunday Times reports that Depop and Facebook Marketplace could also be affected, with officials concerned that messaging features allow adults to contact and potentially groom children.

Research from UK regulator Ofcom found that 19% of children aged eight to 14 use eBay monthly, while 15% use Vinted.

The Children’s Society has also warned that vulnerable children using resale platforms have been targeted by adults offering money in exchange for explicit photographs or attempting to lure them with drugs.

Interestingly, eBay already requires account holders to be at least 18 years old, while Vinted generally has the same minimum age but allows parents or guardians to create supervised accounts for minors.

Canada could restrict Roblox & Minecraft for under 16s as part of social media ban

EU plans sweeping social media and AI chatbot restrictions for kids under 15

UK confirms plans to ban social media for under-16s

The proposals are the latest sign that governments are looking beyond traditional social media when deciding which online platforms children should be allowed to access.

Earlier in 2026, UK officials considered preventing children from talking to strangers in games like Roblox, Fortnite, and Minecraft.

Canada has taken a similar approach, introducing legislation to restrict social media access for under-16s before considering expanding its rules to gaming platforms.

For now, the UK has not confirmed whether online marketplaces will be included in its restrictions, with further details expected as officials finalize which platforms will be affected.

#10841. Toronto couple claim landlord is evicting them for “farting too much”

A Canadian couple and comedy duo are fighting to keep their home after claiming their landlord is trying to evict them over their excessive farting.

S.E. Grummett and Sam Kruger, who perform together as Creepy Boys, have found themselves in a bizarre dispute involving an air-quality monitor at their apartment in Toronto.

The pair claim their landlord believes they’re smoking indoors, causing unusual readings on the device. However, they insist the actual explanation could be something much more embarrassing. Now, they’re raising money to fight their eviction.

In a GoFundMe campaign, the couple explained that the trouble began in December 2025, when their landlord, who lives upstairs, noticed elevated levels of potentially harmful substances in the air.

The two apartments share ventilation, and their landlord was concerned about the readings because of a recently diagnosed lung condition.

Grummett and Kruger said they tried everything from avoiding cooking to showering elsewhere, but the readings continued to rise.

After researching the issue, they discovered that the monitor could be reacting to normal bodily functions, including breathing and, as it turns out, flatulence.

“We are being wrongfully evicted because we fart too much,” they wrote on their fundraiser. “Really. This is not a joke. We wish we were joking.”

However, their landlord reportedly continued to suspect indoor smoking, which the couple denies. They were eventually served an eviction notice stating that the landlord wanted to reclaim their apartment for personal use.

“We believe this to be in bad faith,” they added. “In Ontario, such evictions have to be heard by the Landlord Tenant Board. So, we are going to court.”

The dispute has since reached Ontario’s Landlord and Tenant Board, where a decision remains pending, according to IBTimes.

Meanwhile, the couple’s fundraiser has surpassed its initial CAD $2,800 target, raising more than CAD $3,400 towards legal expenses.

#10837. McDonald’s is letting customers use their rewards points to get cheaper Uber rides

McDonald’s is expanding its rewards program through a new partnership with Uber, allowing customers to redeem their points for discounts on rides.

The fast food giant announced the changes on November 9 as part of a multi-year agreement that will introduce new delivery and loyalty features across 14 markets, including the US, UK, Canada, and Australia.

McDonald’s and Uber first partnered in 2017, with the restaurant chain now available on Uber Eats in 30 countries. The expanded agreement will introduce new ways for customers to earn and spend rewards points and change how deliveries are handled through the McDonald’s app.

Beginning in November 2026, MyMcDonald’s Rewards members in select markets will be able to redeem their accumulated points for discounts on Uber rides through a seasonal promotion in the McDonald’s app.

The companies have not specified which markets will receive the promotion first, how many points will be required for a discount, or the exact date the feature will become available.

Additional rewards features are also planned, including the ability to earn MyMcDonald’s Rewards points on eligible McDonald’s orders placed directly through Uber Eats.

Those points will then be redeemable for rewards and benefits within the McDonald’s app, including future Uber ride offers. However, Uber has not announced a launch date for these additional features.

The agreement also makes Uber McDonald’s exclusive delivery and rideshare rewards partner in participating markets.

Customers will be able to place delivery orders and track their food directly through the McDonald’s app without switching to Uber Eats. Most of these orders will be fulfilled through Uber Direct in partnership with Deliverect.

Amazon Prime members now get free Uber Eats delivery: How to get

McDonald’s slashes price of Quarter Pounder meal with new $8 deal

McDonald’s tests putting ads on menu screens while customers wait for food

Ordering McDonald’s directly through Uber Eats will remain an option.

“By building on our longstanding relationship with Uber, we’re making the customer experience even easier, more reliable and more rewarding, while continuing to give our fans choice in how they order McDonald’s,” said Mason Smoot, McDonald’s Senior Vice President of Global Franchising and Delivery.

The announcement comes just days after Uber Eats announced a new partnership with Amazon, giving Prime members access to free delivery on eligible restaurant orders of $25 or more.

The deal also allows Prime members to order food directly through Amazon’s website or shopping app, with an introductory promotion offering up to $40 off their first two qualifying orders.

Fortnite YouTuber Typical Gamer missed out on the game’s ultra-rare Pirate Shop Token after quitting a match as a joke, only for his teammates to find one moments later.

TG had spent roughly 80 hours searching for the reward, which allows players to claim eligible Fortnite Item Shop cosmetics for free during two separate 14-day periods.

In a TikTok video recounting the incident, Typical Gamer explained that he had been playing Fortnite for around 12 hours a day since the Treasure Hunters season began, searching for the elusive token.

Despite spending days opening chests, he had never found one. That changed when one of his teammates began trolling him, prompting the YouTuber to leave the game as a joke.

“Last game my teammate was trolling me a bit so I left as a joke,” he explained. “In that game, they find the pirate token.”

The video then cuts to footage of the match, with Typical Gamer’s teammates reacting in disbelief after one of them apparently discovers the reward.

“Oh my God, no way,” one teammate repeatedly shouts, while another insists that the discovery is genuine.

“I leave one game, the first game I left in 80 hours of playing, and I don’t get it,” he said. “They literally are in that game. Is that not insane?”

For reference on the item’s rarity, Epic Games confirmed on November 7 that the reward has a one-in-137,500 chance of appearing whenever a player opens a chest during a normal match, although those odds improve during scheduled Treasure Hours.

#10831. 6 Email Tools Developers Can Use to Build, Test, and Monitor Better Email

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password-reset email can leave your application successfully and still fail the user. It might arrive too late, display an unreadable button, or contain a link pointing to the staging environment.

These failures belong to different parts of the email workflow. Fixing the HTML will not repair authentication, and improving sender reputation will not correct an expired verification token.

For developers and designers, the useful question is therefore: which tool helps us inspect the layer that is failing?

The following six options cover mailbox reputation, local testing, message construction, responsive design, compatibility research, and production monitoring. They serve different purposes; they are not interchangeable alternatives.

For teams managing connected business mailboxes, EmailPond describes a service for automated warmup and reputation monitoring, including connections to Google Workspace and Microsoft 365.

The engineering lesson is to distinguish three outcomes:

A successful submission does not establish that a message reached the inbox.

When evaluating mailbox warmup or reputation services, ask what their measurements actually represent. Are they based on participating test accounts, provider feedback, or a proprietary score? What period and sample size do they cover?

Treat any score as one diagnostic input. A result from a testing network is not evidence that every real recipient will receive the same placement.

Also match the tool to your infrastructure. A connected employee mailbox and an application sending through a transactional email provider may use different delivery paths. Activity on one does not demonstrate the health of the other.

Practical exercise: document the sending path for each message type: visible From address, sending provider, authentication domain, and responsible team. This makes later investigations much more precise.

Mailpit runs an SMTP testing server with a web interface and an API for inspecting captured messages. It is useful when testing registration, receipts, invitations, and password resets without delivering development messages to real customers.

Use local capture to inspect more than appearance:

Point the application at SMTP host 127.0.0.1, port 1025, and open http://127.0.0.1:8025 to inspect messages. This example assumes the application runs on the host; an application in another container needs appropriate container networking.

Binding the ports to localhost keeps this development instance off public interfaces. For a repeatable team environment, pin the image to a reviewed version or digest.

Practical exercise: trigger account verification, capture the message, and confirm that the link works once and fails after reuse. Test token behavior through the application, not just by inspecting the email.

Nodemailer provides email-sending functionality for Node.js, including SMTP transport and HTML/plain-text message construction.

Keeping message construction in a dedicated module makes it easier to review and test. Here is a minimal local example using Mailpit:

Run it with node send-test.mjs. Production configuration needs the authentication and TLS settings required by your provider.

SitePoint’s guide to sending email using Node.js provides additional context for building an email workflow.

Practical exercise: move rendering into a separate function and test missing optional fields, long names, and HTML-sensitive characters. Escape untrusted text when inserting it into HTML.

MJML is a markup language that compiles components into responsive email HTML. Its sections, columns, text blocks, and buttons let teams maintain a readable template source.

Keep source templates in version control and compile them during a reproducible build. Review the final output after personalization and any link rewriting.

A component system reduces repetitive layout work, but it does not establish that every message is accessible or renders correctly in every client.

Practical exercise: test a long translated heading, enlarged text, blocked images, and a narrow viewport. The essential message and primary action should remain understandable in each case.

SitePoint’s HTML email design overview offers background on designing within email-client constraints. Pair its established principles with current compatibility data.

Can I Email publishes support information for HTML and CSS across email clients. It helps developers investigate a specific feature before building a template around it.

Start with the clients that matter to your audience. Then inspect support notes for the features your design depends on, including partial support and the date of testing.

A global support percentage cannot tell you whether a feature works for your particular recipients.

For critical content, design a usable fallback. A button should remain identifiable without a decorative background image. A heading should communicate its meaning even when a custom font is unavailable.

Practical exercise: select three visual features in a proposed template and document what users see when each is unsupported. If losing a feature hides the primary action, revise the design.

Compatibility research narrows the risks. Testing the final message in representative clients checks the actual result. SitePoint’s HTML email testing guide provides further background on that review process.

Google Postmaster Tools provides information about email sent to personal Gmail accounts, including authentication, reputation, spam reports, and delivery errors.

Use it alongside application and sending-provider telemetry. Its data concerns Gmail traffic; it is not a universal view of delivery across every mailbox provider.

For example, if delivery errors increase after a provider change, compare the timing with authentication configuration and sending-volume changes. If users report missing messages while the application shows no failures, inspect later delivery events rather than assuming the original SMTP response proves success.

Google’s email sender guidelines explain authentication and sending requirements. SPF authorizes sending infrastructure for the envelope domain, DKIM provides a verifiable signature, and DMARC evaluates alignment with the visible From domain. Configuring them correctly is foundational, but does not guarantee inbox placement.

Practical exercise: create a release checklist that verifies authentication on real test messages from the production sending path, rather than checking DNS records alone.

Tool selection becomes useful when each tool supports a clear acceptance check.

Production systems also need explicit retry behavior. Queueing email can keep slow sending operations out of an HTTP request, but retries can create duplicate messages.

Use a stable application event identifier to track a notification attempt. Where a provider supports idempotency keys, use them according to its documented limits. Otherwise, record provider message identifiers and handle uncertain outcomes carefully: a timeout can occur after the provider has accepted a message.

Test expired notifications as well. A password-reset message delayed in a queue should not promise that its link is still valid.

For delivery-event webhooks, verify authenticity using the provider’s documented mechanism, deduplicate repeated events, and tolerate out-of-order arrival. Avoid logging message bodies or authentication tokens as routine diagnostics.

A useful email workflow gives the team a clear answer to each question: what did we generate, where did we send it, what happened afterward, and could the recipient complete the intended task?

Start with local capture and reliable message construction. Add template and compatibility checks. Then connect production delivery signals to application events.

The measure of success is whether users can verify an account, retrieve a report, or recover access reliably—not simply whether a send function returned successfully.

“I can’t thank the creator of Conan enough!”

Despite the successful escape, the technique shown in Detective Conan is not the recommended first option for escaping a submerged vehicle.

According to AAA, drivers should immediately unbuckle their seat belts and attempt to escape through a window. Waiting for a vehicle to fill with water is extremely dangerous because pressure may not equalize immediately, leaving occupants at risk of drowning.

#10816. 6 Tools for Building Email Automations You Can Test and Debug

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An email automation can fail even when every service reports success.

These are integration problems. They involve state, timing, retries, and ownership—familiar concerns for anyone building web applications.

This guide uses a hypothetical agency dashboard as a running example. The dashboard manages client workspaces, connects to an email platform, records incoming events, and shows operators whether an action is pending, completed, or blocked.

The six tools below address different parts of that workflow. Each section includes an engineering principle and a practical check to help you apply it.

Postman supports JavaScript assertions against API responses. Use it to establish how an integration behaves before connecting it to a production interface.

A successful request is only one part of the contract. Record response shapes, error formats, pagination behavior, and any documented rate limits.

For an application-owned endpoint that acknowledges queued work with HTTP 202, a post-response test could look like this:

This is an illustrative contract for your application, not a claim about an external provider's response format.

The interface should use the operation identifier to show progress. Displaying “completed” immediately after a 202 response would confuse acceptance with completion.

SitePoint’s introduction to building and testing APIs with Postman provides background on organizing requests and tests. Consult current documentation for the latest interface.

Try this: deliberately submit an invalid request and confirm that the dashboard displays a useful error without exposing credentials or raw internal responses.

The useful engineering question is how your application should represent and control these external operations.

Keep provider-specific calls behind an adapter. Application code might call methods such as pauseCampaign() or listReplies(), while the adapter handles authentication, endpoint paths, pagination, and response normalization.

These are application-defined methods, not an assumed vendor SDK.

Workspace boundaries deserve explicit tests. The documentation distinguishes workspace-specific api-user credentials from broader super-admin behavior. Select credentials deliberately, keep them server-side, and bind every operation to a trusted internal workspace mapping.

For webhook processing, use this sequence:

Confirm the available webhook authentication options before implementation; an HTTPS endpoint alone does not establish who sent a request.

Try this: use synthetic data in two test workspaces and confirm that neither credentials nor resource identifiers allow one workspace to read or modify the other.

Webhooks and workers should be designed for repeated delivery. A sender may retry because your acknowledgment was lost, even when the original event reached your application.

A database can enforce a durable deduplication boundary:

Use a stable provider event identifier when available. If none exists, define an event-specific key carefully. A contact ID alone is insufficient because the same contact can generate many legitimate events.

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation explains conflict handling. The receiving application can insert an event with:

The placeholders are bound parameters supplied by the database driver. A returned row indicates a new insertion; no returned row means the key already exists.

This protects event storage from duplicates. It does not automatically make every downstream action execute exactly once.

A worker should apply local state changes and mark the event processed in one database transaction. If the action involves another service, record a durable outgoing operation and use the destination's documented idempotency mechanism where available.

Try this: deliver the same event twice, then restart the worker between attempts. Confirm there is one stored event and no repeated business action.

n8n provides error workflows for handling failed executions. This is useful when integrations combine several steps that operators need to inspect.

For example, a reply might require an application status update and a follow-up task for a human reviewer. Decide which step is authoritative and what should happen if only part of the workflow succeeds.

For this agency dashboard, a reply can pause further automated follow-ups until a person reviews the conversation. That is an explicit business rule, not a universal rule for every email system.

Try this: cause a downstream failure after an earlier step succeeds. Verify that recovery resumes safely instead of repeating the entire workflow blindly.

Mailpit supports captured-message inspection and API-based integration testing. Its testing features can also simulate unexpected SMTP responses.

Use it for emails that your application generates, such as workspace invitations or operational alerts. It does not automatically capture messages sent independently by a remote email platform; those need that platform's test facilities and controlled recipients.

For a workspace invitation, test the full path:

Avoid selecting an unfiltered “latest message” in parallel tests. Another test may have generated a newer message, causing a failure—or a misleading pass.

Keep production recipients and credentials out of test environments. Use unique synthetic addresses and test-only tokens.

SitePoint’s guide to sending email with Node.js offers related background on application-generated mail.

Try this: simulate SMTP failure and confirm the application records a pending or failed operation rather than claiming the invitation was delivered.

Playwright can test the dashboard behavior users depend on: submitting an action, observing progress, correcting errors, and inspecting results.

Define visible states before styling the interface. “Request received,” “waiting for provider,” “completed,” and “needs attention” convey different facts.

An illustrative test for a locally running dashboard might be:

This assumes a configured Playwright base URL and an application that implements the illustrated route, labels, and status region. It verifies presentation behavior against a mock response; a separate integration test must verify actual provider completion.

Designers can review these states alongside developers. Use clear text and accessible status announcements, rather than color alone, to communicate progress and errors.

Playwright's visual comparisons can also detect dashboard layout changes. Browser screenshots do not establish how an email renders in Outlook or Gmail.

SitePoint’s guide to testing Node.js APIs with Jest provides a useful companion for checking backend behavior independently of the interface.

Try this: test a slow response, an expired credential, and a completed operation at a narrow viewport. The next available action should remain clear in each state.

Before releasing an integration, demonstrate these properties:

Measure processing delay, retry counts, and unresolved operations. A high volume of successful HTTP requests can hide an accumulating backlog.

Also provide a controlled reconciliation path for missed events. Where the provider supports it, compare local state with the API using a durable cursor or carefully bounded time window. Passing reconciliation results through the same deduplicated processing path makes recovery easier to reason about.

The strongest automation is one the team can inspect and explain. Each tool should help answer a specific question about correctness, failure, or user experience—and every integration should have a tested route back to a known state.

#10815. A tiny light trap could unlock million qubit quantum computers

After years of slow progress, researchers may finally be seeing a clear path forward in the quest to build powerful quantum computers. These machines are expected to dramatically shorten the time required for certain calculations, turning problems that would take classical computers thousands of years into tasks that could be completed in hours.

A team led by physicists at Stanford University has developed a new kind of optical cavity that can efficiently capture single photons, the basic particles of light, emitted by individual atoms. Those atoms serve as the core components of a quantum computer because they store qubits, which are the quantum equivalent of the zeros and ones used in traditional computing. For the first time, this approach allows information to be collected from all qubits at once.

Optical Cavities Enable Faster Qubit Readout

In research published in Nature, the team describes a system made up of 40 optical cavities, each holding a single atom qubit, along with a larger prototype that contains more than 500 cavities. The results point to a realistic route toward building quantum computing networks that could one day include as many as a million qubits.

"If we want to make a quantum computer, we need to be able to read information out of the quantum bits very quickly," said Jon Simon, the study's senior author and associate professor of physics and of applied physics in Stanford's School of Humanities and Sciences. "Until now, there hasn't been a practical way to do that at scale because atoms just don't emit light fast enough, and on top of that, they spew it out in all directions. An optical cavity can efficiently guide emitted light toward a particular direction, and now we've found a way to equip each atom in a quantum computer within its own individual cavity."

An optical cavity works by trapping light between two or more reflective surfaces, causing it to bounce back and forth. The effect can be compared to standing between mirrors in a fun house, where reflections seem to stretch endlessly into the distance. In scientific settings, these cavities are far smaller and use repeated passes of a laser beam to extract information from atoms.

Although optical cavities have been studied for decades, they have been difficult to use with atoms because atoms are extremely small and nearly transparent. Getting light to interact with them strongly enough has been a persistent challenge.

Rather than relying on many repeated reflections, the Stanford team introduced microlenses inside each cavity to tightly focus light onto a single atom. Even with fewer light bounces, this method proved more effective at pulling quantum information from the atom.

"We have developed a new type of cavity architecture; it's not just two mirrors anymore," said Adam Shaw, a Stanford Science Fellow and first author on the study. "We hope this will enable us to build dramatically faster, distributed quantum computers that can talk to each other with much faster data rates."

Beyond the Binary Limits of Classical Computing

Conventional computers process information using bits that represent either zero or one. Quantum computers operate using qubits, which are based on the quantum states of tiny particles. A qubit can represent zero, one, or both states at the same time, allowing quantum systems to handle certain calculations far more efficiently than classical machines.

"A classical computer has to churn through possibilities one by one, looking for the correct answer," said Simon. "But a quantum computer acts like noise-canceling headphones that compare combinations of answers, amplifying the right ones while muffling the wrong ones."

Scientists estimate that quantum computers will need millions of qubits to outperform today's most powerful supercomputers. According to Simon, reaching that level will likely require connecting many quantum computers into large networks. The parallel light-based interface demonstrated in this study provides an efficient foundation for scaling up to those sizes.

The researchers showed a working 40-cavity array in the current study, along with a proof-of-concept system containing more than 500 cavities. Their next goal is to expand to tens of thousands. Looking further ahead, the team envisions quantum data centers in which individual quantum computers are linked through cavity-based network interfaces to form full-scale quantum supercomputers.

Broader Scientific and Technological Impact

Significant engineering hurdles remain, but the researchers believe the potential benefits are substantial. Large-scale quantum computers could lead to breakthroughs in materials design and chemical synthesis, including applications related to drug discovery, as well as advances in code breaking.

The ability to efficiently collect light also has implications beyond computing. Cavity arrays could improve biosensing and microscopy, supporting progress in medical and biological research. Quantum networks may even contribute to astronomy by enabling optical telescopes with enhanced resolution, potentially allowing scientists to directly observe planets orbiting stars beyond our solar system.

"As we understand more about how to manipulate light at a single particle level, I think it will transform our ability to see the world," Shaw said.

​​Simon is also the Joan Reinhart Professor of Physics & Applied Physics. Shaw is also a Felix Bloch Fellow and an Urbanek-Chodorow Fellow.

Additional Stanford co-authors include David Schuster, the Joan Reinhart Professor of Applied Physics, and doctoral students Anna Soper, Danial Shadmany, and Da-Yeon Koh.

Other co-authors include researchers from Stony Brook University, the University of Chicago, Harvard University, and Montana State University.

This research received support from the National Science Foundation, Air Force Office of Scientific Research, Army Research Office, Hertz Foundation, and the U.S. Department of Defense.

Matt Jaffe of Montana State University and Simon act as consultants to and hold stock options in Atom Computing. Shadmany, Jaffe, Schuster, and Simon, as well as Aishwarya Kumar of Stony Brook, hold a patent on the resonator geometry demonstrated in this work.

Materials provided by Stanford University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10809. Scientists create smart synthetic skin that can hide images and change shape

Synthetic materials are widely used across science, engineering, and industry, but most are designed to perform only a narrow range of tasks. A research team at Penn State set out to change that. Led by Hongtao Sun, assistant professor of industrial and manufacturing engineering (IME), the group developed a new fabrication technique that can produce multifunctional "smart synthetic skin." These adaptable materials can be programmed to perform a wide variety of tasks, including hiding or revealing information, enabling adaptive camouflage, and supporting soft robotic systems.

Using this new approach, the researchers created a programmable smart skin made from hydrogel, a soft, water-rich material. Unlike conventional synthetic materials with fixed behaviors, this smart skin can be tuned to respond in multiple ways. Its appearance, mechanical behavior, surface texture, and ability to change shape can all be adjusted when the material is exposed to external triggers such as heat, solvents, or physical stress.

The findings were published in Nature Communications, where the study was also selected for Editors' Highlights.

Inspired by Octopus Skin and Living Systems

Sun, the project's principal investigator, said the concept was inspired by cephalopods such as octopuses, which can rapidly alter the look and texture of their skin. These animals use such changes to blend into their surroundings or communicate with one another.

"Cephalopods use a complex system of muscles and nerves to exhibit dynamic control over the appearance and texture of their skin," Sun said. "Inspired by these soft organisms, we developed a 4D-printing system to capture that idea in a synthetic, soft material."

Sun also holds affiliations in biomedical engineering, material science and engineering, and the Materials Research Institute at Penn State. He described the process as 4D printing because the printed objects are not static. Instead, they can actively change in response to environmental conditions.

Printing Digital Instructions Into Material

To achieve this adaptability, the team used a method called halftone-encoded printing. This technique converts image or texture data into binary ones and zeros and embeds that information directly into the material. The approach is similar to how dot patterns are used in newspapers or photographs to create images.

By encoding these digital patterns within the hydrogel, the researchers can program how the smart skin reacts to different stimuli. The printed patterns determine how various regions of the material respond. Some areas may swell, shrink, or soften more than others when exposed to temperature changes, liquids, or mechanical forces. By carefully designing these patterns, the team can control the material's overall behavior.

"In simple terms, we're printing instructions into the material," Sun explained. "Those instructions tell the skin how to react when something changes around it."

One of the most eye-catching demonstrations involved the material's ability to conceal and reveal visual information. Haoqing Yang, a doctoral candidate in IME and the paper's first author, said this capability highlights the potential of the smart skin.

To demonstrate the effect, the team encoded an image of the Mona Lisa into the hydrogel film. When the material was washed with ethanol, it appeared transparent and showed no visible image. The hidden image became clear only after the film was placed in ice water or gradually heated.

Yang noted that the Mona Lisa was used only as an example. The printing technique allows virtually any image to be encoded into the hydrogel.

"This behavior could be used for camouflage, where a surface blends into its environment, or for information encryption, where messages are hidden and only revealed under specific conditions," Yang said.

The researchers also showed that concealed patterns could be detected by gently stretching the material and analyzing how it deforms using digital image correlation analysis. This means information can be revealed not only visually, but also through mechanical interaction, adding an extra level of security.

The smart skin also demonstrated remarkable flexibility. According to Sun, the material can easily shift from a flat sheet into complex, bio-inspired shapes with detailed surface textures. Unlike many other shape-changing materials, this transformation does not require multiple layers or different substances.

Instead, the changes in shape and texture are controlled entirely by the digitally printed halftone patterns within a single sheet. This allows the material to replicate effects similar to those seen in cephalopod skin.

Building on this capability, the team showed that multiple functions can be programmed to work together. By carefully designing the halftone patterns, they encoded the Mona Lisa image into flat films that later transformed into three-dimensional forms. As the sheets curved into dome-like shapes, the hidden image slowly appeared, showing that changes in shape and visual appearance can be coordinated within one material.

"Similar to how cephalopods coordinate body shape and skin patterning, the synthetic smart skin can simultaneously control what it looks like and how it deforms, all within a single, soft material," Sun said.

Expanding the Potential of 4D-Printed Hydrogels

Sun said the new work builds on earlier research by the team on 4D-printed smart hydrogels, which was also published in Nature Communications. That earlier study focused on combining mechanical properties with programmable transitions from flat to three-dimensional forms. In the current research, the team expanded the approach by using halftone-encoded 4D printing to integrate even more functions into a single hydrogel film.

Looking ahead, the researchers aim to create a scalable and versatile platform that allows precise digital encoding of multiple functions within one adaptive material.

"This interdisciplinary research at the intersection of advanced manufacturing, intelligent materials and mechanics opens new opportunities with broad implications for stimulus-responsive systems, biomimetic engineering, advanced encryption technologies, biomedical devices and more," Sun said.

The study also included Penn State co-authors Haotian Li and Juchen Zhang, both doctoral candidates in IME, and Tengxiao Liu, a lecturer in biomedical engineering. H. Jerry Qi, professor of mechanical engineering at Georgia Institute of Technology, also collaborated on the project.

Materials provided by Penn State. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10806. AI reads brain MRIs in seconds and flags emergencies

A newly developed artificial intelligence system from the University of Michigan can analyze brain MRI scans and deliver a diagnosis in a matter of seconds, according to a new study. The model identified neurological conditions with accuracy reaching 97.5% and was also able to assess how urgently patients needed medical care.

Researchers say this first-of-its-kind technology has the potential to reshape how brain imaging is handled across health systems in the United States. The findings were published in Nature Biomedical Engineering.

"As the global demand for MRI rises and places significant strain our physicians and health systems, our AI model has potential to reduce burden by improving diagnosis and treatment with fast, accurate information," said senior author Todd Hollon, M.D., a neurosurgeon at University of Michigan Health and assistant professor of neurosurgery at U-M Medical School.

Hollon named the new technology Prima. Over a one-year period, his research team evaluated the system using more than 30,000 MRI studies.

Across more than 50 different radiologic diagnoses involving major neurological disorders, Prima delivered stronger diagnostic performance than other advanced AI models. In addition to identifying disease, the system also proved capable of determining which cases required higher priority.

Certain neurological conditions, including strokes and brain hemorrhages, demand immediate medical attention. Hollon said that in these situations, Prima can automatically alert health care providers so action can be taken quickly.

The system was designed to notify the most appropriate subspecialist, such as a stroke neurologist or neurosurgeon. Feedback becomes available immediately after a patient completes imaging.

"Accuracy is paramount when reading a brain MRI, but quick turnaround times are critical for timely diagnosis and improved outcomes," said Yiwei Lyu, M.S., co-first author and postdoctoral fellow of Computer Science and Engineering at U-M.

"At key steps in the process, our results show how Prima can improve workflows and streamline clinical care without abandoning accuracy."

Prima is classified as a vision language model (VLM), a type of artificial intelligence that can process images, video, and text together in real time. While artificial intelligence has been applied to MRI analysis before, researchers say Prima takes a different approach.

Earlier models were typically trained on carefully selected subsets of MRI data and designed to perform narrow tasks, such as identifying lesions or estimating dementia risk. Prima was trained on a much broader dataset.

Hollon's team used every available MRI collected since radiology records were digitized at University of Michigan Health. This included more than 200,000 MRI studies and 5.6 million imaging sequences. The model also incorporated patients' clinical histories and the reasons physicians ordered each imaging study.

"Prima works like a radiologist by integrating information regarding the patient's medical history and imaging data to produce a comprehensive understanding of their health," said co-first author Samir Harake, a data scientist in Hollon's Machine Learning in Neurosurgery Lab.

"This enables better performance across a broad range of prediction tasks."

Addressing MRI Delays and Radiology Shortages

Each year, millions of MRI scans are performed worldwide, many of them focused on neurological disease. Researchers say the demand for these scans is growing faster than the availability of neuroradiology services.

This imbalance has contributed to staffing shortages, diagnostic delays, and errors. Depending on where a patient receives a scan, results may take days or even longer to return.

"Whether you are receiving a scan at a larger health system that is facing increasing volume or a rural hospital with limited resources, innovative technologies are needed to improve access to radiology services," said Vikas Gulani, M.D. Ph.D., co-author and chair of the Department of Radiology at U-M Health.

"Our teams at University of Michigan have collaborated to develop a cutting-edge solution to this problem with tremendous, scalable potential."

Although Prima performed strongly, researchers emphasize that the work is still in an early evaluation phase. Future research will focus on incorporating more detailed patient information and electronic medical record data to further improve diagnostic accuracy.

This approach mirrors how radiologists and physicians interpret MRIs and other imaging studies in real clinical settings. While artificial intelligence is already used in health care, most existing systems are limited to narrowly defined tasks.

Hollon describes Prima as "ChatGPT for medical imaging," noting that similar technology could eventually be adapted for other imaging types, including mammograms, chest X-rays and ultrasounds.

"Like the way AI tools can help draft an email or provide recommendations, Prima aims to be a co-pilot for interpreting medical imaging studies," Hollon said.

"We believe that Prima exemplifies the transformative potential of integrating health systems and AI-driven models to improve health care through innovation."

Additional authors: Asadur Chowdury, M.S., Soumyanil Banerjee, M.S., Rachel Gologorsky, Shixuan Liu, Anna-Katharina Meissner, M.D., Akshay Rao, Chenhui Zhao, Akhil Kondepudi, Cheng Jiang, Xinhai Hou, Rushikesh S. Joshi, M.D., Volker Neuschmelting, M.D., Ashok Srinivasan, M.D., Dawn Kleindorfer, M.D., Brian Athey, Ph.D., Aditya Pandey, M.D., and Honglak Lee, Ph.D., all of University of Michigan.

Short answer: Pin (pin.com) covers sourcing through scheduling with approvals you control at every step. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and assists recruiters with ranking, outreach, follow-up, scheduling and ATS updates. You approve each step (Assist) or delegate the sequence (Autopilot), and Pin logs every action. Closing and offers stay human.

Be skeptical. Gartner estimated in June 2025 that only about 130 of the thousands of vendors marketing "agentic AI" are real. Test any product on a live role before you pay.

An AI recruiting agent is software that takes a hiring goal, such as "three senior backend engineers in Austin," and works through several steps toward it on its own: finding candidates, ranking them, contacting them, following up and booking interviews. A recruiter sets the brief and the limits and makes the final decisions. The agent assists the recruiter; it does not replace one. See Pin's guide to AI recruiting agents.

A quick test: if you stop prompting it, does the search keep moving? If not, it is a tool.

Demand is rising: in Korn Ferry's October 2025 survey of 1,674 talent leaders, 52% plan to add autonomous agents to their teams in 2026.

Compliance. The employer carries the legal duty. New York City's Local Law 144 requires a bias audit within one year before an automated employment decision tool is used, a public summary of results, and candidate notice at least 10 business days before use. The EU AI Act's Annex III classes AI that filters applications or evaluates candidates as high-risk, and the Commission's timeline puts those obligations at December 2, 2027. Ask each vendor whether its ranking is covered, whether a bias audit exists and what logs it keeps. Pin feeds no names, gender or protected characteristics to its AI, uses third-party fairness audits, and lists an Algorithmic Fairness Report (August 2026) in its trust center on request. This is not legal advice.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform with a built-in agent that assists recruiters across the top of the recruiting funnel, from sourcing to interview scheduling.

Sourcing. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, drawn from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Start from a plain-language brief, a pasted job description, uploaded ideal résumés or an ATS import. Results arrive as a ranked shortlist, each candidate with a fit score and an AI-written reason. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Learn more about AI candidate sourcing.

Assist and Autopilot. In Assist, you delegate tasks one at a time and approve each step. In Autopilot, the agent takes the whole sequence.

In Autopilot, only the email steps send automatically. Pin queues each LinkedIn and SMS step as a task with an AI-drafted message, and the recruiter sends it.

Visibility and learning. Every action appears in a feed, and the agent runs 24/7. Pin's agent page describes an audit log with reasoning, a pause control, approval thresholds for sensitive cases and exclusion lists for past contacts and current employees. The model learns from accept and reject decisions across your team.

Outreach and scheduling. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's profile signals, calibrated to your writing voice. Sequences are multi-channel across email, LinkedIn and SMS: automated email steps plus AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Any reply pauses the sequence, and every channel stays in one thread in a shared team inbox. The Free plan is email-only. MCP server. The Pin MCP server lets an assistant such as Claude or ChatGPT work a Pin job in plain language through ten tools. Accepting a candidate starts outreach from your configured sequence, which Pin confirms every time. It steers Pin from your assistant and is not a separate agent.

#10802. Brain inspired machines are better at math than expected

Computers designed to mimic the structure of the human brain are showing an unexpected strength. They can solve some of the demanding mathematical equations that lie at the heart of major scientific and engineering problems.

In a study published in Nature Machine Intelligence, Sandia National Laboratories computational neuroscientists Brad Theilman and Brad Aimone introduced a new algorithm that allows neuromorphic hardware to solve partial differential equations, or PDEs -- the mathematical foundation for modeling phenomena such as fluid dynamics, electromagnetic fields and structural mechanics.

The results demonstrate that neuromorphic systems can handle these equations efficiently. The advance could help open the door to the first neuromorphic supercomputer, offering a new path toward energy efficient computing for national security and other critical applications.

The research was funded by the Department of Energy's Office of Science through the Advanced Scientific Computing Research and Basic Energy Sciences programs, as well as the National Nuclear Security Administration's Advanced Simulation and Computing program.

Solving Partial Differential Equations With Brain Like Hardware

Partial differential equations are essential for simulating real world systems. They are used to forecast weather, analyze how materials respond to stress, and model complex physical processes. Traditionally, solving PDEs requires enormous computing power. Neuromorphic computers approach the problem differently by processing information in ways that resemble how the brain operates.

"We're just starting to have computational systems that can exhibit intelligent-like behavior. But they look nothing like the brain, and the amount of resources that they require is ridiculous, frankly," Theilman said.

For years, neuromorphic systems were mainly viewed as tools for pattern recognition or for speeding up artificial neural networks. Few expected them to manage mathematically rigorous problems such as PDEs, which are typically handled by large scale supercomputers.

Aimone and Theilman were not surprised by the outcome. They argue that the human brain routinely carries out highly complex calculations, even if people are unaware of it.

"Pick any sort of motor control task -- like hitting a tennis ball or swinging a bat at a baseball," Aimone said. "These are very sophisticated computations. They are exascale-level problems that our brains are capable of doing very cheaply."

Energy Efficient Computing for National Security

The findings could have major implications for the National Nuclear Security Administration, which is responsible for maintaining the nation's nuclear deterrent. Supercomputers used across the nuclear weapons complex consume vast amounts of electricity to simulate the physics of nuclear systems and other high stakes scenarios.

Neuromorphic computing may provide a way to significantly cut energy use while still delivering strong computational performance. By solving PDEs in a brain inspired manner, these systems suggest that large simulations could be run using far less power than conventional supercomputers require.

"You can solve real physics problems with brain-like computation," Aimone said. "That's something you wouldn't expect because people's intuition goes the opposite way. And in fact, that intuition is often wrong."

The team envisions neuromorphic supercomputers eventually becoming central to Sandia's mission of protecting national security.

What Neuromorphic Computing Reveals About the Brain

Beyond engineering advances, the research also touches on deeper questions about intelligence and how the brain performs calculations. The algorithm developed by Theilman and Aimone closely mirrors the structure and behavior of cortical networks.

"We based our circuit on a relatively well-known model in the computational neuroscience world," Theilman said. "We've shown the model has a natural but non-obvious link to PDEs, and that link hasn't been made until now -- 12 years after the model was introduced."

The researchers believe this work could help connect neuroscience with applied mathematics, offering new understanding of how the brain processes information.

"Diseases of the brain could be diseases of computation," Aimone said. "But we don't have a solid grasp on how the brain performs computations yet."

If that idea proves correct, neuromorphic computing might one day contribute to better understanding and treatment of neurological disorders such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's.

Building the Next Generation of Supercomputers

Neuromorphic computing remains an emerging field, but this work represents an important step forward. The Sandia team hopes their results will encourage collaboration among mathematicians, neuroscientists and engineers to expand what this technology can achieve.

"If we've already shown that we can import this relatively basic but fundamental applied math algorithm into neuromorphic -- is there a corresponding neuromorphic formulation for even more advanced applied math techniques?" Theilman said.

As development continues, the researchers are optimistic. "We have a foot in the door for understanding the scientific questions, but also we have something that solves a real problem," Theilman said.

#10800. Sony announces new PlayStation Portal with secret features coming in the future

Sony has announced a new version of the PlayStation Portal featuring additional internal storage, teasing future functionality that has yet to be revealed.

The updated handheld, officially called the PlayStation Portal Player Midnight Black, will launch exclusively in Japan on December 10, 2026. While much of its hardware remains unchanged, Sony has confirmed one addition that could expand what the device is capable of.

In its November 9 announcement on the Japanese PlayStation Blog, Sony confirmed that the updated Portal includes additional internal storage intended to support functionality planned for the future.

“Furthermore, with an eye towards future functionality, the new model includes additional internal storage. We will announce details about this feature as soon as they are finalized,” the company said, according to a machine translation.

Sony did not reveal how much storage the updated handheld includes or explain what the additional capacity will eventually be used for.

The announcement has prompted speculation among PlayStation fans about whether the Portal could eventually support downloading games, running entertainment apps, or playing certain titles without an internet connection.

In a discussion on Push Square, one user questioned whether the additional storage could be used for screenshots or save data, while another suggested it might allow the device to run older PlayStation games locally.

Others have suggested that the storage could support streaming services such as Netflix and YouTube, potentially expanding the Portal’s functionality beyond gaming.

Sony drops massive PlayStation Portal update that finally makes it worth buying

However, Sony has not confirmed any of these possibilities, and there is currently no indication that the handheld will support native PS5 game downloads or offline gameplay.

Alongside the storage addition, Sony is dropping the word “Remote” from the device’s official name, changing it from the PlayStation Portal Remote Player to the PlayStation Portal Player.

The company explained that the name change reflects the handheld’s expanded capabilities following the introduction of cloud streaming, rather than a newly announced feature.

Despite the updated name and additional storage, the new model retains its 8-inch LCD display with 1080p resolution and support for 60 frames per second. It also continues to offer haptic feedback, adaptive triggers, and 3D Audio.

The Japan-exclusive handheld will cost 39,980 yen, approximately $250 USD, and includes a one-month PlayStation Plus Premium subscription. Preorders begin November 13, ahead of its December 10 release.

The device is restricted to Japanese-language settings and requires a PlayStation account registered in Japan, meaning players in other regions cannot simply import it for use with their existing accounts.

Sony has not announced plans to release the updated PlayStation Portal internationally or confirmed whether its mysterious future functionality will also become available on existing models.

#10798. Quantum computer breakthrough tracks qubit fluctuations in real time

Researchers at the Niels Bohr Institute have significantly increased how quickly changes in delicate quantum states can be detected inside a qubit. By combining commercially available hardware with new adaptive measurement techniques, the team can now observe rapid shifts in qubit behavior that were previously impossible to see.

Qubits are the fundamental units of quantum computers, which scientists hope will one day outperform today's most powerful machines. But qubits are extremely sensitive. The materials used to build them often contain tiny defects that scientists still do not fully understand. These microscopic imperfections can shift position hundreds of times per second. As they move, they alter how quickly a qubit loses energy and with it valuable quantum information.

Until recently, standard testing methods took up to a minute to measure qubit performance. That was far too slow to capture these rapid fluctuations. Instead, researchers could only determine an average energy loss rate, masking the true and often unstable behavior of the qubit.

It is somewhat like asking a strong workhorse to pull a plow while obstacles constantly appear in its path faster than anyone can react. The animal may be capable, but unpredictable disruptions make the job much harder.

A research team from the Niels Bohr Institute's Center for Quantum Devices and the Novo Nordisk Foundation Quantum Computing Programme, led by postdoctoral researcher Dr. Fabrizio Berritta, developed a real time adaptive measurement system that tracks changes in the qubit energy loss (relaxation) rate as they occur. The project involved collaboration with scientists from the Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Leiden University, and Chalmers University.

The new approach relies on a fast classical controller that updates its estimate of a qubit's relaxation rate within milliseconds. This matches the natural speed of the fluctuations themselves, rather than lagging seconds or minutes behind as older methods did.

To achieve this, the team used a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a type of classical processor designed for extremely rapid operations. By running the experiment directly on the FPGA, they could quickly generate a "best guess" of how fast the qubit was losing energy using only a few measurements. This eliminated the need for slower data transfers to a conventional computer.

Programming FPGAs for such specialized tasks can be challenging. Even so, the researchers succeeded in updating the controller's internal Bayesian model after every single qubit measurement. That allowed the system to continually refine its understanding of the qubit's condition in real time.

As a result, the controller now keeps pace with the qubit's changing environment. Measurements and adjustments happen on nearly the same timescale as the fluctuations themselves, making the system roughly one hundred times faster than previously demonstrated.

The work also revealed something new. Scientists did not previously know just how quickly fluctuations occur in superconducting qubits. These experiments have now provided that insight.

Commercial Quantum Hardware Meets Advanced Control

FPGAs have long been used in other scientific and engineering fields. In this case, the researchers used a commercially available FPGA based controller from Quantum Machines called the OPX1000. The system can be programmed in a language similar to Python, which many physicists already use, making it more accessible to research groups worldwide.

The integration of this controller with advanced quantum hardware was made possible through close collaboration between the Niels Bohr Institute research group led by Associate Professor Morten Kjaergaard and Chalmers University, where the quantum processing unit was designed and fabricated. "The controller enables very tight integration between logic, measurements and feedforward: these components made our experiment possible," says Morten Kjærgaard.

Why Real Time Calibration Matters for Quantum Computers

Quantum technologies promise powerful new capabilities, though practical large scale quantum computers are still under development. Progress often comes incrementally, but occasionally major steps forward occur.

By uncovering these previously hidden dynamics, the findings reshape how scientists think about testing and calibrating superconducting quantum processors. With current materials and manufacturing methods, moving toward real time monitoring and adjustment appears essential for improving reliability. The results also highlight the importance of partnerships between academic research and industry, along with creative uses of available technology.

"Nowadays, in quantum processing units in general, the overall performance is not determined by the best qubits, but by the worst ones: those are the ones we need to focus on. The surprise from our work is that a 'good' qubit can turn into a 'bad' one in fractions of a second, rather than minutes or hours.

"With our algorithm, the fast control hardware can pinpoint which qubit is 'good' or 'bad' basically in real time. We can also gather useful statistics on the 'bad` qubits in seconds instead of hours or days.

"We still cannot explain a large fraction of the fluctuations we observe. Understanding and controlling the physics behind such fluctuations in qubit properties will be necessary for scaling quantum processors to a useful size," Fabrizio says.

Materials provided by University of Copenhagen. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
