#10793. Building Location-Aware Interfaces Without Mixing Up Language, Currency, and Region

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor opens an online store while traveling. Their browser uses English, their account is registered in Canada, their network appears to be in Germany, and their delivery address is in France.

Which currency should the store show? Which products are available? What language should the checkout use?

There is no single answer hidden inside a country code. These decisions depend on different signals and different product rules.

For developers and designers, the challenge is to represent that context explicitly. Otherwise, a convenient shortcut—such as deriving everything from an IP address—can produce confusing prices, unexpected redirects, and incorrect availability.

This article walks through a practical approach to building a regional product interface, with examples that also apply to travel sites, service directories, and applications that retrieve current web information.

Avoid a single variable named country that silently controls every aspect of the interface.

These values can disagree without any of them being wrong.

For an anonymous visitor, network region might help suggest an initial market. Once the user selects a destination, the application should not silently replace it because a later request arrives from a different network.

Write precedence rules for each decision. Language selection and delivery eligibility need separate policies.

SitePoint’s introduction to JavaScript internationalization provides useful background on separating software capabilities from locale-specific adaptation.

This is an illustrative application contract. It keeps the observation's market, currency, and timestamp attached to the value.

Without that context, another component might reuse the amount for a different market or label it as current long after availability changes.

At the API boundary, validate the required fields and their allowed values. A missing amount must not become zero, and a missing availability value must not become “in stock.”

Also define the timestamp precisely. Does it mean the source was fetched, the source published an update, or your database last processed the record? Those are different events.

For the interface, use wording consistent with the evidence: “Checked at 09:00” is more precise than “Live availability” when you only have a timestamped observation.

Browser preferences and network location operate at different layers.

Changing the browser locale can affect language preferences and formatting. It does not relocate the connection seen by the server. Similarly, supplying browser geolocation coordinates does not change the network's public IP address.

This distinction also matters in live-web retrieval. Bixberry describes an opt-in network that routes requests through participants' internet connections. It illustrates how retrieval infrastructure can involve connections outside a conventional data-center environment.

When comparing two responses, record the relevant conditions:

Keep diagnostic records minimal and avoid retaining unnecessary personal information.

For authorized regional testing of your own application, SitePoint’s guide to testing geo-dependent web apps offers related background on separating browser settings from network behavior.

JavaScript's Intl.NumberFormat formats numeric values according to locale and currency settings:

Both calls represent the same euro amount. Formatting it for a different locale does not convert it to another currency.

Do not turn a euro price into a dollar price by changing only the currency argument. A conversion requires a defined exchange-rate source, timestamp, rounding policy, and explanation of whether the result is an estimate or the actual charged amount.

For calculations, choose a representation appropriate to your pricing model rather than relying on floating-point arithmetic throughout. If using integer minor units, remember that currencies do not all use two decimal places.

SitePoint’s overview of the JavaScript Internationalization API explains related formatting capabilities.

Practical check: switch interface language while leaving the selected market unchanged. The number format may change, but the underlying price and currency should remain consistent with the product rules.

A country flag is a weak substitute for a language label. Countries can have multiple languages, and the same language can be used across many countries.

Use clear, separate controls when the product needs them:

Do not expose a control unless its effect is meaningful and implemented consistently. A currency selector that updates a price label but leaves checkout charging behavior unexplained creates confusion.

Before a market change, explain consequential effects. For example:

Changing your delivery country may update availability and delivery options.

Preserve the user's task where possible. If a product is unavailable in the newly selected market, retain its page and show an explanation instead of sending the user to an unrelated homepage.

For designers, test longer translations, currency codes, and expanded validation messages. Use logical CSS properties where appropriate so layouts can adapt to right-to-left content:

These rules are a starting point, not a complete localization solution. Review reading order, icons, numeric content, and translated copy with people familiar with the target language.

A regional interface can behave correctly at the origin and still show incorrect content when a shared cache serves the wrong variant.

If a response varies by market or language, that variation must be reflected in your cache design. One option for public content is an explicit route structure:

These routes are illustrative. They make market and language differences visible, but the rest of the cache configuration must still match the application's behavior.

Personalized prices and account-specific responses require their own cache policy. A public page cache must not expose one account's content to another.

Test the deployed caching layer, not only a local development server:

Keep freshness requirements separate from variant separation. A response can belong to the correct market and still be too old.

Playwright's emulation settings let tests configure locale, time zone, geolocation, and permissions.

This assumes a configured base URL and an application implementing the illustrated route and selectors. It tests the application's handling of explicit market selection under a different locale; it does not prove that the request originated in Germany.

Use controlled API fixtures for predictable frontend tests, then test real routing and caching separately. Browser emulation is one layer of verification, not a substitute for every regional check.

A location-aware application should be able to explain why it chose a language, price, destination, or availability result.

Keep those decisions separate, preserve the context of retrieved data, and make explicit user choices easy to change. Then test the situations where signals disagree.

The result is an interface that works for travelers, multilingual households, remote workers, and anyone whose preferences cannot be reduced to the location of their current internet connection.

#10792. Call of Duty conversation sparks bomb scare forcing plane and airport terminal to evacuate

A Call of Duty player’s conversation caused a bomb scare at a California airport, forcing passengers to evacuate their plane and part of the terminal before police realized it was all a misunderstanding.

According to CBS Los Angeles, a passenger overheard someone discussing a bomb during a FaceTime conversation and immediately alerted the flight crew.

The passenger reportedly heard the words “bomb” and “explosion,” prompting authorities to take the potential threat seriously.

As a precaution, officials evacuated the aircraft and cleared the east wing of Terminal 2 while police investigated the situation.

Police detained the individual responsible for the suspicious comments, but the situation quickly took an unexpected turn.

While being questioned, the passenger explained that the conversation was actually about Call of Duty, with the references to explosives relating to events happening in the video game.

What initially sounded like a potentially dangerous situation turned out to be someone discussing virtual explosions with a friend.

Authorities conducted a sweep of the aircraft but found no explosives or other suspicious devices. Investigators ultimately determined that no crime had been committed.

All passengers, including the individual who had been detained, were eventually allowed to reboard the flight and continue their journey.

It’s unclear how long the evacuation delayed the flight or where the aircraft was headed.

#10791. ChatGPT as a therapist? New study reveals serious ethical risks

As more people seek mental health advice from ChatGPT and other large language models (LLMs), new research suggests these AI chatbots may not be ready for that role. The study found that even when instructed to use established psychotherapy approaches, the systems consistently fail to meet professional ethics standards set by organizations such as the American Psychological Association.

Researchers from Brown University, working closely with mental health professionals, identified repeated patterns of problematic behavior. In testing, chatbots mishandled crisis situations, gave responses that reinforced harmful beliefs about users or others, and used language that created the appearance of empathy without genuine understanding.

"In this work, we present a practitioner-informed framework of 15 ethical risks to demonstrate how LLM counselors violate ethical standards in mental health practice by mapping the model's behavior to specific ethical violations," the researchers wrote in their study. "We call on future work to create ethical, educational and legal standards for LLM counselors -- standards that are reflective of the quality and rigor of care required for human-facilitated psychotherapy."

The findings were presented at the AAAI/ACM Conference on Artificial Intelligence, Ethics and Society. The research team is affiliated with Brown's Center for Technological Responsibility, Reimagination and Redesign.

Zainab Iftikhar, a Ph.D. candidate in computer science at Brown who led the study, set out to examine whether carefully worded prompts could guide AI systems to behave more ethically in mental health settings. Prompts are written instructions designed to steer a model's output without retraining it or adding new data.

"Prompts are instructions that are given to the model to guide its behavior for achieving a specific task," Iftikhar said. "You don't change the underlying model or provide new data, but the prompt helps guide the model's output based on its pre-existing knowledge and learned patterns.

"For example, a user might prompt the model with: 'Act as a cognitive behavioral therapist to help me reframe my thoughts,' or 'Use principles of dialectical behavior therapy to assist me in understanding and managing my emotions.' While these models do not actually perform these therapeutic techniques like a human would, they rather use their learned patterns to generate responses that align with the concepts of CBT or DBT based on the input prompt provided."

People regularly share these prompt strategies on platforms like TikTok, Instagram, and Reddit. Beyond individual experimentation, many consumer facing mental health chatbots are built by applying therapy related prompts to general purpose LLMs. That makes it especially important to understand whether prompting alone can make AI counseling safer.

Testing AI Chatbots in Simulated Counseling

To evaluate the systems, the researchers observed seven trained peer counselors who had experience with cognitive behavioral therapy. These counselors conducted self counseling sessions with AI models prompted to act as CBT therapists. The models tested included versions of OpenAI's GPT Series, Anthropic's Claude, and Meta's Llama.

The team then selected simulated chats based on real human counseling conversations. Three licensed clinical psychologists reviewed those transcripts to flag possible ethical violations.

The analysis uncovered 15 distinct risks grouped into five broad categories:

The Accountability Gap in AI Mental Health

Iftikhar noted that human therapists can also make mistakes. The key difference is oversight.

"For human therapists, there are governing boards and mechanisms for providers to be held professionally liable for mistreatment and malpractice," Iftikhar said. "But when LLM counselors make these violations, there are no established regulatory frameworks."

The researchers emphasize that their findings do not suggest AI has no place in mental health care. Tools powered by artificial intelligence could help expand access, particularly for people who face high costs or limited availability of licensed professionals. However, the study highlights the need for clear safeguards, responsible deployment, and stronger regulatory structures before relying on these systems in high stakes situations.

For now, Iftikhar hopes the work encourages caution.

"If you're talking to a chatbot about mental health, these are some things that people should be looking out for," she said.

Ellie Pavlick, a Brown computer science professor who was not involved in the research, said the study underscores the importance of carefully examining AI systems used in sensitive areas like mental health. Pavlick leads ARIA, a National Science Foundation AI research institute at Brown focused on building trustworthy AI assistants.

"The reality of AI today is that it's far easier to build and deploy systems than to evaluate and understand them," Pavlick said. "This paper required a team of clinical experts and a study that lasted for more than a year in order to demonstrate these risks. Most work in AI today is evaluated using automatic metrics which, by design, are static and lack a human in the loop."

She added that the study could serve as a model for future research aimed at improving safety in AI mental health tools.

"There is a real opportunity for AI to play a role in combating the mental health crisis that our society is facing, but it's of the utmost importance that we take the time to really critique and evaluate our systems every step of the way to avoid doing more harm than good," Pavlick said. "This work offers a good example of what that can look like."

Materials provided by Brown University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10786. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Nintendo’s Switch 2 remake launched on November 5, bringing a host of improvements to the 1998 classic, including a dedicated jump button that gives players more control over Link’s movement.

As we highlighted in our Ocarina of Time remake review, the new mechanics make exploring Hyrule better than ever.

However, it took speedrunners just a couple of days to discover that Nintendo’s new movement system could be exploited in ways the developers almost certainly never intended.

As reported by Polygon and IGN, one of the biggest discoveries is a technique called “actor jumping,” which allows Link to launch himself absurdly high into the air.

The glitch involves standing on a moving object, such as a Magic Bean platform or movable gravestone, using an item like the Hookshot, and carefully timing the targeting and jump inputs.

When performed correctly, Link goes flying, allowing players to bypass obstacles and reach locations far earlier than intended.

In one demonstration, a speedrunner shows how the technique can be used to access the Shadow Temple shortly after obtaining the Hookshot, allowing players to bypass the Forest, Fire, and Water Temples on their way to the Shadow Temple.

This could allow players to obtain the Hover Boots early, making subsequent sections of the Forest, Fire, and Water Temples easier to navigate.

#10781. Scientists discover AI can make humans more creative

Artificial intelligence (AI) is commonly viewed as a technology designed to automate work and potentially replace human labor. However, new research from Swansea University offers a different perspective. The findings suggest that AI can also function as a creative collaborator that encourages exploration, engagement, and inspiration.

Researchers from the University's Computer Science Department carried out one of the largest studies so far examining how people work alongside AI during creative design tasks. More than 800 participants joined an online experiment where they used an AI-supported system to design virtual cars.

Rather than quietly optimizing designs behind the scenes, the system used a method called MAP-Elites to produce visual galleries filled with many different design possibilities. These galleries showed a wide spectrum of car concepts, including highly effective designs, unusual ideas, and even some intentionally flawed options.

Turing Fellow Dr. Sean Walton, Associate Professor of Computer Science and the study's lead author, explained: "People often think of AI as something that speeds up tasks or improves efficiency, but our findings suggest something far more interesting. When people were shown AI-generated design suggestions, they spent more time on the task, produced better designs and felt more involved. It was not just about efficiency. It was about creativity and collaboration."

Why Traditional AI Evaluation May Be Too Limited

The study, published in the ACM journal Transactions on Interactive Intelligent Systems, also highlights a problem with how AI design tools are typically assessed. Standard metrics often focus on simple behaviors, such as how frequently users click on or copy AI suggestions. According to the researchers, these measures overlook important aspects of the experience, including how the technology influences people's thoughts, emotions, and willingness to explore new ideas.

The Swansea researchers argue that AI systems should be evaluated using broader methods that capture these deeper effects. Understanding how AI shapes human thinking and engagement could provide a more complete picture of its impact.

Dr. Walton emphasized that variety in AI-generated output played a crucial role in the experiment. "Our study highlights the importance of diversity in AI output. Participants responded most positively to galleries that included a wide variety of ideas, including bad ones! These helped them move beyond their initial assumptions and explore a broader design space. This structured diversity prevented early fixation and encouraged creative risk-taking.

"As AI becomes increasingly embedded in creative fields, from engineering and architecture to music and game design, understanding how humans and intelligent systems work together is essential. As the technology evolves, the question is not only what AI can do but how it can help us think, create and collaborate more effectively."

Materials provided by Swansea University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10778. A Practical Workflow for Testing JavaScript Forms Before Deployment

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Web forms are an essential part of many applications. They allow users to register accounts, submit contact requests, update profiles, and complete purchases. However, a form that appears to work correctly during development can still fail when users enter unexpected values, lose their connection, or submit information multiple times.

Testing forms before deployment helps developers identify these problems early. Instead of relying only on a successful submission, it is useful to test validation, browser behavior, accessibility, and error handling as separate parts of the workflow.

Before writing tests, define what the form should do from the user's perspective. A registration form, for example, might require a valid email address, a password that meets specific rules, and confirmation that the user has accepted the terms.

List the expected outcomes for each field. Consider valid input, empty values, incorrect formats, and unusually long entries. This gives you a clear checklist for testing the form without depending on assumptions about how users will interact with it.

It is also worth considering what happens after submission. Does the form display a confirmation message? Are users informed when something goes wrong? Can they correct their input without entering everything again?

These details help establish the expected behavior before testing begins.

HTML provides several built-in attributes that handle common validation requirements. The required attribute prevents submission when a mandatory field is empty, while type="email" checks whether an email address follows an accepted format.

This approach reduces the amount of JavaScript needed for basic validation. It also provides a useful starting point for testing browser behavior.

However, client-side validation should not be treated as a security measure. Users can bypass browser checks, so the server must validate submitted data independently before processing or storing it.

Developers can also explore SitePoint's HTML and responsive web design guide to understand how form elements fit into layouts across different screen sizes.

JavaScript becomes useful when a form requires custom rules or more detailed feedback. For example, a password field might need to meet a minimum length, or two fields might need to contain matching values.

When testing this logic, check both successful and unsuccessful cases. A common mistake is to test only valid submissions, leaving error conditions unexamined.

Make sure that error messages explain what users need to change. A message such as "Please enter a valid email address" is more useful than a generic notification stating that the submission failed.

Also check that validation does not unexpectedly clear fields or prevent users from correcting mistakes. Error messages should be associated with the relevant inputs so that keyboard and assistive technology users can identify the problem.

Browser developer tools help identify problems that are not immediately visible in the interface. The Console panel can reveal JavaScript errors, while the Network panel helps developers inspect requests sent when a form is submitted.

For example, a form might display a loading indicator but never show a result because its API request returns an error. Inspecting the request status, response, and timing can help distinguish a frontend problem from a backend failure.

The MDN guide to browser developer tools explains how to inspect page elements, debug JavaScript, and investigate browser behavior.

Test situations such as an unavailable server, a delayed response, and invalid server-side data. The interface should communicate the problem clearly and provide a reasonable next step.

A form should remain usable across different devices and input methods. Test it with a keyboard to confirm that users can move between fields, activate buttons, and identify validation errors without a mouse.

Every input should have a clear label. Placeholder text alone is not a reliable replacement because it disappears when users begin typing.

Next, test the form at several viewport widths. Check that labels remain readable, buttons are accessible, and fields do not extend beyond their containers. Long error messages should not overlap other elements or break the page layout.

Automated accessibility checks can identify some issues, but manual testing is still important for understanding the actual user experience.

A simple checklist makes form testing more consistent across projects:

Check server errors and slow network responses.

Confirm that repeated submissions are handled appropriately.

Test keyboard navigation and accessible error messages.

Verify the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

For larger applications, these checks can be supplemented with automated unit and integration tests. Automation is particularly useful when the same validation rules must work consistently across multiple forms.

Reliable form testing involves more than confirming that a submit button works. Developers need to consider validation, server responses, accessibility, responsive layouts, and unexpected user behavior.

By combining HTML validation, targeted JavaScript tests, browser developer tools, and a repeatable checklist, teams can identify problems before deployment. The result is a more dependable interface that helps users complete tasks without unnecessary confusion or frustration.

#10775. AI-powered robot learns how to harvest tomatoes more efficiently

Farm labor shortages are pushing agriculture toward greater automation, especially when it comes to harvesting. But not all crops are easy for machines to handle. Tomatoes, for example, grow in clusters, which means a robot must carefully select ripe fruit while leaving unripe ones untouched. This requires precise control and smart decision-making.

To tackle this challenge, Assistant Professor Takuya Fujinaga of Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Engineering developed a system that trains robots to assess how easy each tomato is to harvest before attempting to pick it.

His approach combines image recognition with statistical analysis to determine the best angle for picking each fruit. The robot analyzes visual details such as the tomato itself, its stems, and whether it is hidden behind leaves or other parts of the plant. These inputs guide the robot in choosing the most effective way to approach and pick the fruit.

From Detection to "Harvest-Ease" Decision-Making

This method shifts away from traditional systems that focus only on detecting and identifying fruit. Instead, Fujinaga introduces what he calls "harvest-ease estimation." "This moves beyond simply asking 'can a robot pick a tomato?' to thinking about 'how likely is a successful pick?', which is more meaningful for real-world farming," he explained.

In testing, the system achieved an 81% success rate, exceeding expectations. About one-quarter of the successful picks came from tomatoes that were harvested from the side after an initial front-facing attempt failed. This indicates the robot can adjust its approach when the first attempt is not successful.

The research underscores how many variables affect robotic harvesting, including how tomatoes cluster, the shape and position of stems, surrounding leaves, and visual obstruction. "This research establishes 'ease of harvesting' as a quantitatively evaluable metric, bringing us one step closer to the realization of agricultural robots that can make informed decisions and act intelligently," Fujinaga said.

Future of Human-Robot Collaboration in Farming

Looking ahead, Fujinaga envisions robots that can independently judge when crops are ready to be picked. "This is expected to usher in a new form of agriculture where robots and humans collaborate," he explained. "Robots will automatically harvest tomatoes that are easy to pick, while humans will handle the more challenging fruits."

The findings were published in Smart Agricultural Technology.

Is the U.S. Department of Commerce simply rubber stamping these applications?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An investigation has found that addresses for two Chinese companies approved to purchase Nvidia H200 chips led to empty offices, and the receptionists at the buildings were unfamiliar with them. According to The New York Times, these companies didn’t just have empty offices; their executives are often linked to other firms sanctioned by the United States for working with and providing advanced technology to the Chinese military.

This news has revealed concerns that the U.S. Department of Commerce is simply rubber-stamping GPU export license applications instead of scrutinizing each one for potential links to Beijing. “I have a ton of concerns that neither the Commerce Department or Nvidia is doing the due diligence needed to ensure that the licensing terms are being followed,” former China director for the White House National Security Council Liza Tobin told the publication.

Many of these approved companies include well-known entities like Baidu, BYD, Tesla Shanghai, as well as ZTE, which has previously been blacklisted from the U.S. market, Alibaba, Tencent, and ByteDance. However, the outfit’s investigation also revealed approval for two relatively unknown firms: New Matrix and Maginfra. The offices for both companies were empty shells with some office furniture, and people working nearby said they’d never heard of the company or seen anyone go to the deserted spaces.

The New Matrix website also reportedly did not contain any information about its owners or its history, although public company records reveal that it was founded by Li Bin, who, along with two other primary owners, are connected to Wise Road, a Chinese private equity firm sanctioned by the U.S. for helping Beijing acquire sensitive chip technologies. Furthermore, Mr. Li chairs Tsinghua Unigroup, a tech conglomerate partly owned by the Chinese government, and owns New H3C Semiconductor Technologies, which has also been added to the Commerce Department’s Entity List for cooperating with the Chinese military.

Maginfra, on the other hand, is suspected of being linked to Inspur, which Washington has blacklisted since 2023 for supporting the People’s Liberation Army, with both companies sharing personnel, patents, and a similar government ownership structure. These two firms aren’t listed as the final users of Nvidia’s AI chips, though. Instead, they’re supposed to assemble them into AI servers, which would then be forwarded to a final user, although it’s unclear who their customers are.

Aside from these questionable non-entities, another company that received a license to purchase H200 GPUs has some suspicious links. Zhejiang Yindun Cloud Technology’s executives are reportedly related to the founder of Range Intelligent Computing Technology Group, a data center operator that has sold compute capacity to Chinese military contractors. It’s also reportedly partly operating out of a Range data center.

President Donald Trump banned the export of Nvidia AI GPUs in April 2025, even those customized to meet the performance ceiling Washington set for AI processors. The president reversed the decision late last year in exchange for a 25% cut of the sale, but it still took more than eight months for the first H200 shipments to arrive in China. Beijing’s refusal to give Chinese tech companies the green light to buy the chips for the first few months of the year caused the delay. But once China lifted its import ban on Nvidia chips, several Chinese companies applied for licenses to buy them, and The New York Times reported that around two dozen firms have already received approval, including these questionable ones.

#10771. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."

#10767. 7 Ways to Keep API Changes Safe as More Teams Join a Project

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A backend team renames a response field from name to displayName. Its tests pass, the deployment succeeds, and the API continues returning HTTP 200. Minutes later, a dashboard starts showing blank user labels.

The failure is easy to explain: the server changed a contract that another application depended on. Preventing it requires more than asking teams to communicate better. The dependency needs to become visible in documentation, tests, and deployment decisions.

This article follows that field rename through seven practical safeguards. The examples apply to frontend and backend teams working in the same office, across departments, or in different countries.

Start by documenting the existing response and the intended replacement.

The two objects may look equivalent to a person, but software reads specific keys. A client using user.name will not automatically discover displayName.

An OpenAPI description can document request and response shapes alongside status codes and other interface details. Keep it in version control and update it in the same change as the implementation.

Document meaning as well as structure. Does displayName contain user-supplied text? Can it be empty? Is it intended for display only? Must consumers avoid using it as an identifier?

SitePoint’s REST API best practices provides background on interface design. For an existing API, the immediate task is to identify the promises clients already rely on.

An endpoint can serve more than the application shown in the product demo. Consumers may include an administrative dashboard, a mobile app, scheduled exports, internal scripts, and external integrations.

The point is to discover different upgrade timelines. A web application may update quickly, while an installed mobile client remains active for months.

Inspect request telemetry where available, but do not treat a short period of inactivity as proof that a consumer is unused. A quarterly export will not appear in a one-week observation window.

Also record whether consumers reject unknown fields. Adding a response property is often easier to migrate than removing one, but strict decoders can make even additive changes incompatible.

For this example, a transitional response can expose both fields:

Generate both values from the same source so they cannot drift during the migration.

The fourth step needs evidence. The server usually cannot observe which property a client reads after receiving a response. Use consumer version information, published contracts, maintained integration records, and explicit confirmations where appropriate.

For external APIs, removal may require a new version or a documented deprecation process. Do not remove a field simply because the internal dashboard has migrated.

Consider rollback before deployment. If the consumer now requires the new field, rolling the server back to an older response can break it. Either preserve compatibility during rollback or give the consumer a deliberate transition strategy.

A temporary adapter can let a consumer accept both the old and transitional response formats. Keep it narrow and document when it can be removed.

Save the following runnable example as user-label.test.mjs:

Run it with node --test user-label.test.mjs.

The fallback policy is a product decision. For a navigation label, “Unnamed user” may be acceptable. For an operation that requires a verified identity, missing required data may need to stop the workflow.

These tests exercise the adapter only. They do not prove that the live API returns the expected payload or that the application calls the adapter. Add integration coverage at the boundary where the response enters the client.

For UI rendering, insert the returned label as text or use your framework’s normal escaping. The adapter is not an HTML sanitizer.

Several tools can contribute to the review, but their responsibilities differ.

A useful pipeline can lint the API description, compare it against the released baseline, run consumer tests, and verify provider behavior.

Store the baseline deliberately. Comparing two files from the same proposed change can miss a regression against the deployed contract.

SitePoint’s Postman workflow guide explains collections and request organization. Keep shared examples free of production credentials, and include failure responses alongside successful ones.

Mocks should include old, transitional, and new payloads during a migration. This gives frontend developers and designers a way to inspect loading states, missing data, and error handling before the provider rollout finishes.

As more contributors join a project, architectural decisions need to remain understandable outside the meeting where they were made.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

For a distributed project, the engineering requirement is concrete: a new contributor should be able to identify who owns an interface, which consumers depend on it, and how a change reaches production.

Use actual team names and version identifiers in a real repository. Link the note from the pull request and make unresolved decisions explicit.

A reviewer should not need to infer whether a field is already deprecated or whether the mobile team has been consulted. That context should remain available when colleagues work different schedules.

Define what success looks like before shipping. For this rename, useful checks might include schema-validation failures, client exceptions around user rendering, and unexpected increases in missing-name fallbacks.

Avoid putting names or entire response bodies into diagnostic logs. A template version, consumer version, error category, and correlation identifier may provide sufficient evidence.

Test combinations that can exist during deployment:

Do not assume every combination is supported forever. Define the supported window and its rollback implications.

Once the removal condition is satisfied, retire the old field and temporary adapter through reviewed changes. Update fixtures and documentation so they describe the current contract.

A safe API migration gives teams an explicit sequence, tests the compatibility assumptions, and preserves enough evidence to decide when the transition is complete. That is what allows contributors to work independently without surprising the applications that depend on them.

#10764. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

#10759. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10758. NASA is hiring astronauts who could live and work on the Moon

NASA is looking for its next generation of astronauts, and you could be among those selected to travel to the Moon or even help prepare for humanity’s first trip to Mars.

The space agency has officially opened applications for its latest astronaut recruitment drive, offering successful candidates the chance to work on some of its biggest upcoming missions.

It’s a pretty well-paid job, too. The position comes with an estimated annual salary of $164,395, although there’s a fairly demanding list of requirements.

NASA is hoping to recruit people who could eventually set foot on the Moon, help establish a permanent lunar base, or contribute to future missions to Mars.

“This is an extraordinary time to join NASA’s astronaut corps,” said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman in a November 7 announcement.

“The people we select could walk on the Moon, live and work at the Moon Base, and help pioneer the systems and operations that will ultimately carry crews to Mars.”

Anyone who believes they have what it takes can submit an application through the official job listing. But before you start packing your bags for space, there are a few boxes you’ll need to tick.

Firstly, applicants must be US citizens and typically need a master’s degree in a science, technology, engineering, or mathematics (STEM) field. However, certain medical qualifications, doctoral studies, and test pilot training can also meet the education requirements.

Candidates also need at least three years of relevant professional experience, although pilots can qualify through specific flight-hour requirements.

They must also pass NASA’s medical examinations and demonstrate strong communication, teamwork, and leadership skills.

Those selected will undergo approximately two years of training, learning everything from spacewalking and robotics to operating spacecraft.

However, completing the training doesn’t guarantee a trip into space. The competition will be fierce, as during its previous recruitment drive in 2024, more than 8,000 people applied, but just 10 were selected.

NASA is accepting applications until December 3, 2026, with its next astronaut class expected to be selected in late 2027.

#10753. 7 Checks That Make Form Bugs Easier to Reproduce Across Teams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer reports that a form’s error message overlaps its submit button. The developer opens the same page and sees no problem. Both are using the same code, but one has a narrow viewport, a translated interface, and an unusually long validation message.

A screenshot records what appeared on screen. A reproducible example also preserves the inputs, responses, permissions, and environment that produced it. That distinction matters when people review work at different times or join a project without knowing its undocumented conventions.

This article uses a workspace-invitation form as an example. The form accepts an email address and a role, submits a request, and displays either a confirmation or an error. The seven checks below turn its awkward states into something developers and designers can inspect together.

“The form looks broken” leaves too much for the next person to discover.

A useful report specifies the starting state, action, observed result, and expected behavior:

Include the browser, application revision, and relevant account permissions. Explain whether “360 pixels” means the browser’s layout viewport or the dimensions of an exported screenshot.

Avoid using real customer addresses in shared fixtures. A synthetic address such as member@example.test is sufficient to exercise the interface.

This report gives a designer a clear layout requirement and gives a developer a precise state to recreate.

A form tested only with successful submissions is missing most of its interesting behavior.

Create a small set of fixtures that cover the product’s actual rules:

Keep fixture values deterministic. Random addresses or constantly changing timestamps make visual comparisons harder to interpret.

Record expected outcomes alongside the data. A response containing a message does not establish where the interface should display it, whether focus should move, or which fields should remain editable.

Treat those as product decisions and test them explicitly.

Some failures can be reproduced by passing props to a component. Others depend on the request lifecycle.

Storybook args can describe inputs for isolated component states. An error-state story might supply a message, current field values, and a pending flag to a presentational form component.

However, passing pending: true does not prove that the real application enters or exits that state correctly.

For network-dependent behavior, use Mock Service Worker or your test runner’s request interception to supply a controlled response. Preserve the distinction between these two checks:

Avoid mocking the same endpoint in several overlapping layers. When a response is unexpected, the team should be able to identify which fixture produced it.

Also keep at least some integration coverage against the real API. A frontend mock that returns an outdated error shape can make the interface look reliable while hiding a broken contract.

Open the failing state at the recorded viewport. Inspect the error container, its parent layout, and the submit button.

Look for constraints such as fixed heights, absolute positioning, hidden overflow, and a flex or grid item that refuses to shrink.

A possible layout for a message beside an action is:

This pattern allows the message column to shrink and stacks the elements at a narrow width. It is not a universal fix: choose a breakpoint and arrangement that suit the surrounding form.

If the error belongs to a particular field, placing it beside the submit button may be the wrong design regardless of how well it wraps.

SitePoint’s browser-based CSS debugging guide explains how to inspect applied rules and layout behavior. Its guide to troubleshooting CSS with Inspect Element provides additional background.

Use DevTools experiments to identify the cause, then make the lasting change in the source. Check a wide viewport afterward so a narrow-screen fix does not introduce a desktop regression.

A screenshot can show that an error is present. It cannot, by itself, prove that the form preserved the email address or prevented an unintended navigation.

The following Playwright example assumes an existing application route, configured baseURL, and accessible form labels. Adapt the endpoint, response schema, and selectors to your project.

The example expects a native select for the role and a named form landmark. A custom role picker requires different interactions.

The behavior assertions precede the visual comparison. The screenshot then helps reviewers spot overlap, clipping, or unintended spacing changes.

Playwright’s visual comparison documentation explains baseline screenshots. Generate and compare them in a consistent environment; operating-system and font differences can affect rendering.

Review the first baseline manually. A screenshot test can faithfully preserve a bug if the initial image is approved without inspection. Disabling animation for a snapshot also does not test the accessibility of motion itself.

As a project grows, a component’s behavior should remain discoverable without a private walkthrough.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

The practical requirement for distributed contributors is a review process that carries its own context. Attach the exact fixture, preview location, reproduction command, and expected behavior to the issue or pull request.

Keep the open decision separate from the implemented behavior. This prevents reviewers from assuming that a visual change has already resolved a product question.

Name the person or team responsible for the component, and identify where design decisions are recorded. A stable fixture is useful only if someone maintains it as the component evolves.

A form that no longer overlaps can still be difficult to use.

Verify visible labels, keyboard navigation, focus visibility, and the relationship between errors and their fields. For field-level errors, consider aria-invalid and aria-describedby where appropriate.

The alert in the test example assumes a newly displayed form-level error. Do not add several competing live regions or announce every keystroke. Check the resulting experience with assistive technology.

SitePoint’s accessible form layout guide is relevant background for the relationship between structure and usability.

A focused toolset can support the workflow:

Use the smallest set that answers the current question. Adding another tool is useful when it closes a testing gap, not merely because it produces another report.

A good handoff lets someone unfamiliar with the original conversation recreate the failure, understand the expected behavior, and verify the fix. When the fixture and regression check remain with the code, that work continues to help the next contributor.

#10751. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

#10745. Nintendo is hiring someone to preserve its old games and consoles

Nintendo is looking to hire someone to help preserve its gaming history, as more and more classic titles risk being lost to time.

Video game preservation has become an increasingly important issue, especially as older consoles become obsolete and digital storefronts shut down. With many games never receiving modern re-releases, some are becoming virtually impossible to play without tracking down their original hardware.

In fact, a 2023 study found that 87% of classic games released in the US were no longer commercially available.

Now, Nintendo is taking steps to ensure that important parts of its own gaming history are preserved for future generations.

According to its official job listing, the company is searching for a Digital Archivist, who will help preserve technical records and development materials from previous generations.

“We are seeking a Digital Archivist, Historical Systems to support the preservation, organization, and accessibility of historical hardware and software information,” the description reads.

The successful applicant will be responsible for organizing historical documents, creating a searchable database, and interviewing long-serving employees to record their knowledge.

Nintendo says the goal is to ensure information about its older hardware and software remains accessible to its engineering, development, and research teams.

The position is based in Redmond, Washington, and pays between $35 and $55 per hour on a 12-month contract. Applicants need a relevant bachelor’s degree or equivalent experience, alongside at least two years working in areas such as digital archiving or records management.

However, while the move could help safeguard Nintendo’s history, it doesn’t necessarily mean that more classic games will become available to players.
