#10716. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

Materials provided by University of British Columbia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10714. Valorant 13.10 update bring Sentinel buffs and big changes to assists

Valorant’s 13.10 update is here, with some buffs and nerfs for different Sentinels, as well as a change for in-game assists. So, lets get into the patch notes.

Valorant has undergone some big updates over the last few months, including the arrival of Hush in 13.09, a new rifle in the form of the Warden coming in 13.06, and a raft of Agent ability changes in the 13.00 patch.

Now, with the 13.10 update rolling out, Riot Games has, again, put Sentinels under the microscope with changes coming to a number of those Agents, specifically: Cypher, Killjoy, and Vyse.

Now, these three agents will have a shorter cooldown when it comes to their abilities coming back into play after being destroyed. Cypher’s Spycam, Killjoy’s Turret, and Vyse’s Arc Rose will all take 45 seconds to regenerate.

“We’re making some light changes to Sentinel cooldowns to help encourage more strategic back and forth between teams. This is a small, targeted change while we continue to look at ability cooldowns and Sentinel states across VALORANT more broadly,” Riot said.

We’re decreasing to a shorter cooldown after these abilities are destroyed to see if it gives Sentinels a bit more room to place utility early and reset mid-round, while still rewarding the team that breaks their setup.”

However, the reclaim cooldown on Cypher’s Spycam has been increased from 15 seconds to 20 seconds, bringing it in-line with other Sentinels.

Sage mains will also notice a small change too, with Riot increasing her accuray when targeting allies for healing.

Additionally, healing assists will now only count when a player you’ve healed gets a kill within a 10 second window after the heal effect ends.

Similarly, damage assists have been tweaked, meaning you’ll need to deal at least 10 damage to an enemy within three seconds of your teammate bagging a kill.

The full patch notes for the 13.10 update can be found below.

#10713. How to Organize CSS with Cascade Layers in Large Web Projects

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

As a website grows, its stylesheet often becomes harder to maintain. A rule added to fix one component can unexpectedly affect another, and developers may find themselves adding increasingly specific selectors to override previous styles. Over time, these changes make CSS difficult to understand and update.

CSS cascade layers provide a way to organize styles into named groups and control their priority. Instead of relying entirely on selector specificity and the order of declarations, developers can establish a predictable structure for their stylesheets.

CSS determines which declarations apply by considering factors such as origin, importance, cascade layer priority, specificity, and source order. When several rules target the same element, understanding these factors helps explain why one style overrides another.

If all three rules apply to the same button under normal author styles, the ID selector wins because it has greater specificity.

This can become inconvenient when a project contains third-party styles, reusable components, and application-specific overrides. Developers may respond by adding more specific selectors, making future changes increasingly difficult.

Cascade layers offer another approach: establish a clear priority order before writing individual component rules.

A useful starting point is to divide styles according to their purpose. For example, a project might contain reset styles, third-party styles, reusable components, and page-specific adjustments.

You can establish these layers at the beginning of your stylesheet:

The declaration at the top establishes the order for normal declarations in these layers. For normal author styles, later layers have higher priority than earlier layers, regardless of selector specificity between those layers.

This means a simple selector in a higher-priority layer can override a more specific selector in a lower-priority layer.

For more background on building adaptable interfaces, developers can explore SitePoint's introduction to CSS container queries, which explains another way to structure maintainable component styles.

Not every project needs the same number of layers. A small website may require only a few, while a large application may benefit from a more detailed structure.

This is a suggested structure rather than a mandatory convention. The important part is defining the priority order intentionally and applying it consistently.

Cascade layers can help avoid selectors that become unnecessarily complicated.

For normal declarations, the second rule wins because overrides has higher layer priority, even though the first selector contains an ID and more class selectors.

This can make changes easier to review because developers do not need to keep increasing selector specificity to achieve the intended result.

However, layer order should not become an excuse to place every exception in a global override layer. Keep related styles close to their components and use overrides only when the exception is intentional.

Cascade layers have rules that developers need to understand before adopting them across an existing codebase.

For normal declarations, later layers have higher priority. For declarations marked !important, the layer priority is reversed. Important declarations in earlier layers take precedence over important declarations in later layers.

Unlayered normal author styles also take precedence over normal declarations inside author cascade layers. This matters when integrating a layered stylesheet with older CSS that remains outside the layer structure.

The MDN documentation on CSS cascade layers explains these priority rules and provides additional examples.

Before reorganizing a production stylesheet, test how existing third-party rules, inline styles, and important declarations interact with the new structure.

Rewriting an entire stylesheet at once can introduce regressions. A safer approach is to define the intended layer order, migrate one group of styles at a time, and compare the rendered interface after each change.

Start with styles that are easy to isolate, such as resets or a small set of reusable components. Check buttons, forms, navigation, and responsive layouts at several viewport sizes.

Browser developer tools can help identify which declaration is winning and whether it belongs to the expected layer. Visual regression tests are also useful when the application has many shared components.

For teams, documenting the layer order and agreeing on where new styles belong can prevent the stylesheet from becoming disorganized again.

CSS cascade layers provide developers with a practical way to manage style priority in growing applications. By separating resets, vendor styles, components, and utilities into a clear hierarchy, teams can reduce specificity conflicts and make stylesheets easier to maintain.

The best results come from a deliberate layer structure, consistent naming, and careful testing during migration. Cascade layers do not remove the need to understand CSS specificity, but they give developers another tool for controlling how styles interact.

#10709. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.

ALL.Net, a contraction of Amusement Linkage Live Network, was launched by Sega in 2004 to allow arcade games to look up to the net for auto updates, data logging, implement online play, and power national leaderboards. There is currently no estimate for issue resolution, but a news bulletin is promised for November 10 (Tuesday).

Classic offline arcade machines weren’t affected by this outage, of course. Moreover, rival arcade gaming legends like Taito and Konami run their own, separate, arcade networks and authentication systems.

#10705. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10704. Japan making AI app to learn Japanese by talking to anime characters

Japan is developing a new language-learning app that will let anime fans practice their Japanese by having AI-powered conversations with characters from their favorite shows.

Anime and manga have become hugely popular around the world, introducing millions of people to Japanese culture and encouraging many to learn the language themselves.

Now, the Japanese government is hoping to capitalize on that popularity with an app that makes learning Japanese feel more like interacting with your favorite characters.

As reported by The Japan Times, Japan’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs has asked the Japan Foundation to develop an AI-powered language-learning app aimed primarily at younger audiences overseas.

Rather than simply studying vocabulary or completing exercises, users would be able to practice speaking Japanese by having conversations with characters from popular anime and manga.

While no specific characters or franchises have been announced just yet, it would likely mean users could converse with the likes of Goku from Dragon Ball Z or Monkey D. Luffy from One Piece.

The Japan Foundation has previously developed language-learning tools featuring original characters, but licensing restrictions have prevented it from using recognizable faces from existing franchises. For the new app, it plans to negotiate with copyright holders to secure permission to use famous characters.

The government hopes the app will encourage more people to learn Japanese while also making lessons accessible in countries where qualified teachers and dedicated language schools are harder to find.

It could also give anime fans a new way to engage with the characters they love, although they’ll have to wait to find out which ones will be available.

#10702. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10699. UK Government cracking down on phones being used in schools from next year

The UK government is set to introduce “tougher” measures against phones being used in schools after warnings from teachers and school leaders.

Over the years, mobile phones have become more commonplace in schools. Students need them to contact parents when the day is over, and they’re sometimes used during interactive parts of lessons as well.

However, some schools see them more as a disruption. As a result, a number of schools across the UK have introduced systems where students place their phones into sealed bags at the start of the day and aren’t allowed to open them until they have free time, or the school day has ended.

Yet, in 2027, phones might be completely banned from schools following “tougher” measures from the government.

As per the BBC, the government is set to ban phones from being in students’ bags or pockets throughout the day.

This, they say, is a “tougher” measure than the current “no see, no hear” policy that schools operate with regard to phones. Plenty of schools are fine with phones being in students’ pockets or bags as long as they’re not seen or heard during lessons.

According to Education Secretary Lucy Powell, the new policy will help show that “school is for learning and friendship, not scrolling” and access to phones “risks pulling children away from their learning.”

These rules are separate from the impending social media ban for under 16s, which has affected a number of social platforms. Under those rules, teenagers will even have access restricted to sites like eBay and Facebook Marketplace in order to keep them safe.

UK considering banning under 16s from eBay to “keep children safe”

UK plans to restrict voice chat in games for under-16s

UK set to ban playing loud music on public transport

UK officials have also considered preventing children from talking to strangers in games like Roblox, Fortnite, and Minecraft.

Similar stances have been taken in Australia and Canada too, with the Australian government facing lawsuits over it.

The European Union has also considered sweeping social media restrictions for under 16s as well, but they have not come into law yet.

This is a great gaming mouse in terms of performance, but it's currently the same price as the prettier G502 X Plus.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Logitech's G502 X Lightspeed has been around for awhile — the mouse was introduced in 2022 alongside the fancier Logitech G502 X Plus (and the wired G502 X), but since it’s receiving a firmware update that upgrades its Hero 25K sensor to a Hero 44K sensor, this seemed like a good time to take a look at it. The G502X Lightspeed is available in black and white colorways and currently retails for $159.99, which is the same price as the G502 X Plus — however, it can (and should) normally be found for less than this.

The Logitech G502 X Lightspeed is a right-handed gaming mouse with an ergonomically contoured shape. It's the same size and shape as the G502 X Plus, with large primary mouse buttons, textured rubber side panels for grip, and a smooth matte chassis with glossy accents. It comes in black and white colorways (our review unit was black) and has no lighting aside from a small indicator light at the front of the mouse to indicate connection and battery life.

Like the G502 X Plus, the G502 X Lightspeed is definitely on the larger side. It measures 5.17 inches (131.4mm) long by 3.12 inches (79.2mm) wide, and is 1.62 inches (41.1mm) high at its tallest point. It's larger than the Razer Basilisk V3 Pro and V4 Pro (5.11 x 2.96 x 1.67 inches / 130 x 75.4 x 42.5mm) — not by a lot, but it definitely feels like a much larger mouse. It weighs about 3.6 ounces (102g), which makes it lighter than the Basilisk V3 Pro, but slightly heavier than the Basilisk V4 Pro.

The G502 X Lightspeed has 11 programmable buttons, and 13 programmable controls if you include scroll up/down. There are the left and right primary mouse buttons, as well as two side buttons along the left edge of the left mouse button. On the left side of the mouse there are the two standard thumb buttons, plus a "sniper" button that's magnetically attached and can be flipped so that it's not in your way. The sniper button can also be "deleted," and Logitech includes a delete cover in the box that makes the button unpressable (and also has the same soft, rubbery texture as the rest of the thumb rest).

The scroll wheel is a 4-way tilt wheel, so it has three programmable "buttons" (left click, right click, and center click), as well as scroll up/down. Above the scroll wheel are two buttons: One is a mechanical scroll wheel switch that switches between notched, tactile scroll and free scroll — that’s not programmable, as it's a physical switch — and the other is a profile cycle button that can be remapped.

On the bottom of the mouse you'll find UHMWPE mouse feet — at the top and bottom, around the sensor, and under the thumb rest. There's a power switch, as well as a magnetically-attached dummy puck that can be swapped out for the charging puck included in the Logitech PowerPlay 2. Under the puck is a slot for storing the mouse's wireless dongle.

In the box, the G502 X Lightspeed comes with a 6-foot (1.8m) USB-C-to-USB-A cable, a USB-A dongle, a USB-C converter, and a sniper button delete cover.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

5.17 x 3.12 x 1.62 inches / 131.4 x 79.2 x 41.1 mm

The Logitech G502 X Lightspeed ships with Logitech's Hero 25K optical sensor, which has a max resolution of 25,600 DPI and a maximum speed of 400 IPS, and can handle up to 40 G's of force. However, both the G502X Lightspeed and the G502 X Plus are receiving a firmware update that will upgrade the Hero 25K optical sensor to a Hero 44K optical sensor, with a maximum resolution of 44,000 DPI and a maximum speed of up to 678 IPS. It's not clear when this upgrade will roll out, however, and I wasn't able to upgrade my review unit in G Hub as of this writing.

Upgrades aside, the G502 X Lightspeed's sensor is very good. It tracks on just about every surface you might possibly use while gaming, and is speedy, sensitive, and very accurate. The mouse supports a polling rate of up to 1,000 Hz, which is still pretty standard for most gamers — however, if you're someone who's convinced that the extra 0.125 milliseconds of lag is the reason for your losses, this is not a mouse with an 8,000 Hz or even a 4,000 Hz polling rate.

The G502 X Lightspeed features Logitech's Lightforce hybrid optical-mechanical switches in its primary mouse buttons, which combine the feel of mechanical switches with the speed and reliability of optical ones. The G502 X Lightspeed's switches do have a nice, tactile click with a crisp sound that's definitely on the louder side. The side buttons also feel and sound good — not quite as tactile as the primary buttons, but with a very crisp click sound.

The mouse features a dual-mode tilt wheel with a mechanical switch that lets you move between a notched, tactile scroll and a smooth, speedy free-scroll. Logitech's implementation of this is different from Razer's — Logitech's is mechanical, while Razer's is software-based. This means the modes are more distinct, but I'm not really sure if that's a good thing. While I liked the free-scroll mode of the G502 X Lightspeed better than I liked the free-scroll mode on Razer's mice (such as the Basilisk V4 Pro and the Naga V3 Pro), the tactile scroll was stiff, clunky, and very loud — with a lot of rattling. The scroll wheel only has two modes, so there's no middle ground — and while I'm fine to use free-scroll for most things, there are definitely games where I need a notched scroll wheel.

The G502 X Lightspeed is configurable via Logitech's universal peripheral software, G Hub. You can use G Hub to change the mouse's DPI settings (DPI steps and DPI shift speed — the DPI switch that occurs when you hit the DPI shift button) and remap keys. You can also use G Hub to update the mouse's firmware, so this is presumably where the Hero 44K sensor upgrade will come through.

The G502 X Lightspeed connects to your PC via 2.4GHz wireless; it can also utilize a wired connection, though the included USB-C cable is rubber and won't get you that smooth, drag-free wired mouse movement you can get with flexible paracord-covered mouse cables. The mouse does not include Bluetooth.

Logitech rates the G502 X Lightspeed at 140 hours of battery life, which is about 10 hours longer than the G502 X Plus (with no lighting). This is pretty impressive, even if there are similar wireless mice that last longer (the recently-launched Razer Basilisk V4 Pro gets up to 200 hours, for example). This should give you at least two weeks before you need to recharge, even with intense use. The G502X Lightspeed is also compatible with the PowerPlay 2, Logitech's wireless charging mousepad (sold separately).

The G502 X Lightspeed is a solid gaming mouse for most gamers — its contoured, ergonomic shape is comfortable, though it does feel bigger than similarly shaped mice, such as the Razer Basilisk V4 Pro. It has enough extra well-placed buttons for the average gamer (though, not enough for the average MMO player), and its 140-hour battery life is higher than average. Plus, assuming it actually gets this firmware update, it will have a competitive sensor — though it's not going to be quite as good as the actual Hero 2 sensor. But this mouse is currently priced at $159.99, which is the same price as the fancier Logitech G502 X Plus — and it's definitely supposed to be the more budget version of the two. Unless you find it at a great sale price, I'd pick up the G502 X Plus or even Razer's Basilisk V3 Pro 35K at a similar price.

#10697. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

#10694. Warzone players blocked from multiplayer for using apps to find easy lobbies

Warzone began shutting players out of multiplayer in Season 1 if they ran a paid app that scouted lobby skill levels.

The update, which went live on November 6, folded Modern Warfare 4 into the battle royale and swapped Verdansk and Rebirth Island for Hajin and Zodiac. It was a smorgasbord of new maps, guns, and perks, built to give everyone a clean slate. Some players, however, had already paid to skip the hard part.

Tucked into the November 5 patch notes, Activision labeled Warzone Agent, a paid third-party app, as unauthorized software that violated its Terms of Use.

The way the agent worked is players would copy the lobby ID shown in the bottom-left corner of the screen during pre-match warm-ups and entered it on Warzone Agent’s website, which returned the lobby’s average K/D ratio.

A low score signaled an easy crowd, while a high one gave them a reason to duck out and keep searching, a habit better known as lobby shopping. According to its site, the service cost $39.99 a year.

Under the new rules, RICOCHET Anti-Cheat flags anyone who launches Warzone with the app running and tells them to close it. Players who refuse lose access to Multiplayer modes, although Campaign stays open. Meanwhile, anyone who tries to slip around the block risks temporary or permanent account and hardware bans, and a hardware ban isn’t some small fry, since it can shut an entire device out of the game.

Warzone Agent never offered aimbots or wallhacks, and the devs acknowledged as much. Instead, it gave users intel the rest of the lobby simply didn’t have. Still, the patch notes stated that “any software that provides an unfair advantage undermines the experience for other players.”

Warzone players bash “useless” update that does nothing to fight cheaters

Warzone devs reportedly remove “2-box” exploit at last

Warzone is nerfing SBMM in Season 1 but players aren’t convinced

The Call of Duty team doubled down in a thread on X the next day, saying it was acting against players willing to pay for “underhanded methods of gaining an edge.” It also pledged to keep going after tools and behaviors that compromise Warzone’s integrity.

The crackdown marked yet another chapter in Warzone’s long tug-of-war over matchmaking. Last year, Raven Software eased skill-based matchmaking with Open Matchmaking in Season 1, though plenty of players doubted skill had really taken a back seat.

#10693. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10691. Generating Web-Ready Images With AI: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming an important part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product visuals, blog illustrations, backgrounds, and other digital content. However, creating an image is only the first step. Preparing it for efficient web delivery is equally important.

Unoptimized images can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay page rendering. These problems can affect Core Web Vitals, especially Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques, including image resizing, modern formats, Node.js automation, responsive delivery, and performance testing.

Images often represent a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. When developers upload large images without processing them, visitors may experience slower loading times, particularly on mobile networks.

Large image files that take longer to download.

Incorrect dimensions for different screen sizes.

Missing image dimensions that cause layout shifts.

Excessive compression that reduces visual quality.

Unnecessary downloads caused by poor responsive configuration.

A consistent optimization workflow helps address these problems while maintaining the intended visual appearance.

Before preparing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width hero banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small article illustration.

These are suggested dimensions, not fixed requirements. The actual size depends on the website layout and the devices being supported.

When preparing source visuals, developers can compare different AI image tools to understand how image generation workflows handle aspect ratios, output dimensions, and visual details.

Regardless of the generation method, keep an original high-quality version. This master file can be used to create smaller versions without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Choosing an appropriate image format can reduce file size while maintaining acceptable visual quality.

AVIF: Provides efficient compression for many photographs and detailed illustrations.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and environments requiring broad compatibility.

PNG: Suitable for screenshots, lossless graphics, and images that require transparency.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare the resulting file sizes and inspect image quality before selecting a format.

For technical details, consult the MDN Web Docs guide to image file formats.

Manually resizing multiple images can become time-consuming. Automation provides a consistent way to generate different image dimensions and formats.

Sharp is a Node.js library that supports resizing, compression, and image conversion.

The script generates WebP and AVIF versions at several widths.

The quality values are starting points. Adjust them after checking the output for compression artifacts, loss of detail, and unnecessary file size.

For additional configuration options, refer to the official Sharp documentation.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on available resources and screen layout.

The HTML srcset attribute provides multiple image candidates, while sizes describes the expected rendered width.

The browser uses the available image candidates and layout information to select a suitable resource.

Make sure the sizes attribute accurately reflects the actual website layout. Incorrect values can cause browsers to download larger images than necessary.

Responsive delivery is particularly useful when the same visual appears in different layouts across desktop, tablet, and mobile devices.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide alternative image formats with a fallback.

The browser selects a supported source, while the img element provides fallback behavior and alternative text.

Always verify that the referenced files exist and that their dimensions match the intended layout.

Images without defined dimensions can cause page content to move while resources are loading. This can negatively affect Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Include width and height attributes whenever possible:

The browser can use the dimensions to reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

Images below the initial viewport can also use native lazy loading:

However, avoid lazy-loading the main hero image when it contributes to LCP. Important above-the-fold images should be loaded promptly.

Reducing file size should not significantly affect the visual details required by the website.

This is particularly important for images containing:

A practical quality-checking process includes:

Compare the optimized image with its original version.

Inspect the image at its actual display dimensions.

Test the output on mobile and desktop screens.

The smallest file is not necessarily the best option. A slightly larger image may provide noticeably better visual quality.

After implementing the optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Open Chrome DevTools and select the Network panel. Filter requests by images and reload the webpage.

Mobile devices receive appropriately sized images.

The browser downloads unnecessary image files.

Modern formats are being served correctly.

Important images load without unnecessary delays.

Image dimensions match their display requirements.

You can inspect the image source selected by the browser using:

For websites containing multiple images, inspect each relevant element.

Finally, run an audit using Google PageSpeed Insights. It can help identify oversized images and other performance issues.

The web.dev guide to optimizing LCP also provides useful technical guidance for diagnosing loading delays.

Remember that image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, and JavaScript execution can also influence page loading.

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one image format always provides the best compression.

Using incorrect responsive image settings.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Ignoring the actual image selected by the browser.

Measuring file size without checking visual quality.

A proper testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but results depend on image content, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best approach.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of a webpage should generally load promptly.

The fundamental optimization techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, generated visuals may contain complex textures and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design, but efficient delivery requires more than simply exporting and uploading a visual.

Choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive delivery. Include image dimensions to prevent layout shifts, prioritize important visual elements, and test the results across different devices.

By following these practices, developers can maintain visual quality, reduce unnecessary downloads, and create faster, more reliable website experiences.

#10688. GTA 6 confirms Xbox Cloud Gaming support but PC players face a catch

Rockstar Games has confirmed that Grand Theft Auto 6 will support Xbox Cloud Gaming when it launches on November 19, allowing players to experience the highly anticipated title without running it directly on their consoles.

The announcement comes as pre-loading officially begins for players who have digitally pre-ordered GTA 6. Alongside new details about the game’s performance on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X/S, Rockstar has outlined how cloud streaming and Remote Play will work.

While Xbox Cloud Gaming support will extend access to older hardware, Rockstar’s announcement specifically limits the feature to Xbox consoles, leaving PC players without a cloud gaming option at launch.

In its November 10 announcement, Rockstar confirmed that GTA 6 will be available through Xbox Cloud Gaming on Xbox Series X/S and Xbox One consoles.

“The game will also be available via XBOX Cloud Gaming on XBOX Series X|S and XBOX One consoles, utilizing XBOX Series S graphics settings,” the developer explained.

This means Xbox One owners can play GTA 6 through cloud streaming, even though the game won’t receive a native release on Microsoft’s previous-generation console.

Instead of running the game locally, Xbox One users can stream it from Microsoft’s cloud infrastructure, provided they have access to the service and a suitable internet connection.

To access it, owners will need to own the game and then purchase access to Xbox Cloud Gaming either through a Game Pass subscription or by buying hours.

Rockstar also confirmed that the cloud version will use Xbox Series S graphics settings, regardless of which supported Xbox console players use to access it.

GTA 6 boss sends warning to pirates who make AI-generated PC version before official release

Former Rockstar dev says threat of unofficial AI ports could force GTA 6 onto PC sooner

“And as with all cloud streaming, connection speeds and other technical factors may influence game performance in ways that would not impact local gameplay,” the company added.

Notably, Rockstar’s announcement makes no mention of Xbox Cloud Gaming support on PC, and Xbox’s Matthew Ball confirmed the game won’t stream to PC. At least not at launch.

Although cloud gaming support is limited to consoles, Rockstar also confirmed that GTA 6 will support Remote Play on both PlayStation and Xbox.

“GTA VI will also fully support Remote Play on PlayStation and XBOX,” the developer stated.

Unlike cloud gaming, Remote Play allows players to stream games directly from their own consoles to another compatible device over an internet connection or local network.

This could potentially allow GTA 6 owners to stream the game to a Windows PC through Xbox Remote Play or to a PC or Mac using Sony’s PS Remote Play application.

It could also open the door to playing GTA 6 on handheld devices such as the Steam Deck and ROG Xbox Ally X20 through compatible Remote Play software.

However, Rockstar has not specifically confirmed PC or handheld compatibility for GTA 6’s Remote Play functionality, meaning those possibilities remain unverified.

Even if Remote Play works on PC, players would still need to own a compatible console running GTA 6, rather than accessing the game directly through a native PC version or Microsoft’s cloud servers.

GTA 6 launches November 19 for PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X/S, with no native PC release date announced.

It has taken 25 years to get here and 300mm diamond wafers are in its sights for 2030.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Diamonds have exceptional cooling capabilities due to their excellent thermal conductivity, with the material estimated to be at least five times more conductive than copper. Because of this, Diamond Foundry, a firm focusing on lab-grown diamonds, announced the world’s first 200mm single-crystal diamond (SCD) wafer. The 200mm SCD is about the same wafer size that semiconductor fabs use for older processes, with the company boasting on its website that “Single-crystal diamond has entered the wafer-scale era.” This is part of the effort to manufacture SCD at scale so that it can be used for semiconductor cooling, especially now that increasing power of AI chips has also led to enormous thermal problems.

What makes Diamond Foundry’s 200mm SCD wafer different from other lab-grown diamonds is that it’s created as a high-quality single-crystal piece instead of a polycrystalline gem. Although the former is difficult to manufacture, it is what allows diamond to become an extreme efficient thermal conductor while simultaneously retaining its electrical insulating properties — this efficiency should help cut down on hotspots, making SCDs an excellent solution for AI chip cooling.

The company also showed in a graph that it is now thought possible to create a D300 SCD wafer before 2030, matching the size of the wafers used in leading node processes. However, it should be noted that the sample shown by the company remains unpolished, meaning it still needs to go through some processes to make it suitable for chipmakers.

Although the company was founded in 2012, it also acknowledged the work of scientists who have been chipping away at the scale problem of SCDs since the early 2000s. “This is one of those milestones that once seemed almost impossible. Hundreds of DF engineers, scientists, and technicians contributed to us getting here. It is the accumulated result of countless experiments, failures, inventions, rebuilt machines, and insights over more than a decade,” Diamond Foundry CEO Martin Roscheisen said.

“Stefan and Martin pursued this goal for 25 years. There are very few examples in technology of someone sticking to a really hard scientific challenge with that level of perseverance. They kept going when the prevailing view was that single‑crystal diamond simply could not be made at this scale. Today, the perseverance shows as the first-ever D200mm single‑crystal diamond. And best of all, it makes D300mm predictable as well.”

Diamond Foundry isn’t the only company that has identified the potential of lab-grown diamonds for semiconductor cooling. Akash Systems secured $18.2 million in CHIPS Act funding in 2024 to produce Gallium Nitride fused with synthetic diamond, which it claims will reduce temperatures by 20 degrees C. The De Beers Group, which is known for its diamond mines in Africa, is also building a copper-plated diamond composite material aimed at improving cooling efficiency for AI, high-performance computing, and GaN RF devices. Even Frore Systems, which is known for its solid-state cooler, is diving into diamond-infused watercooling cold plates for Nvidia’s next-generation AI GPUs.

#10685. NASA’s new AI space chip could let spacecraft think for themselves

NASA is developing a powerful new computer chip designed to dramatically increase the intelligence and performance of future spacecraft. Through a commercial partnership, the project is creating advanced processing technology capable of helping spacecraft operate more independently during missions far from Earth.

NASA's High Performance Spaceflight Computing project is focused on boosting the computing capabilities of spacecraft used in space exploration. Current missions rely on older processors because they are durable enough to survive the extreme conditions of space. While those chips are dependable, they lack the performance needed for more advanced missions.

The agency says newer and far more capable processors are essential for future autonomous spacecraft, faster onboard scientific analysis, and supporting astronauts during missions to the Moon and Mars.

"Building on the legacy of previous space processors, this new multicore system is fault-tolerant, flexible, and extremely high-performing," said Eugene Schwanbeck, program element manager in NASA's Game Changing Development program at the agency's Langley Research Center, in Hampton, Virginia. "NASA's commitment to advancing spaceflight computing is a triumph of technical achievement and collaboration."

Radiation Hardened Processor Faces Extreme Testing

At the center of the project is a new radiation-hardened processor built to deliver up to 100 times the computing power of today's spaceflight computers while surviving the harsh environment of space. Engineers at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California are running a wide range of tests designed to simulate those conditions.

"We are putting these new chips through the wringer by carrying out radiation, thermal, and shock tests while also evaluating their performance through a rigorous functional test campaign," said Jim Butler, High Performance Space Computing project manager at JPL.

To qualify for spaceflight, the processor must withstand intense electromagnetic radiation and dramatic temperature fluctuations that can damage electronics. High-energy particles from the Sun and deep space can also trigger computer errors that force spacecraft into "safe mode," temporarily shutting down nonessential systems until engineers resolve the issue.

NASA is also testing how the chip handles the challenges of planetary landings.

"To simulate real-world performance, we are using high-fidelity landing scenarios from real NASA missions that would typically require power-intensive hardware to process huge volumes of landing-sensor data," said Butler. "This is an exciting time for us to be working on hardware that will enable NASA's next giant leaps."

Testing at JPL began in February and is expected to continue for several months. Early results have been highly encouraging. According to NASA, the processor is functioning as intended and has shown performance levels roughly 500 times greater than the radiation-hardened chips currently used in spacecraft.

The team also marked the beginning of testing with a symbolic moment by sending an email titled "Hello Universe," referencing the famous introductory messages used during the early days of computer programming.

AI Powered Spacecraft and Deep Space Missions

The processor is being developed jointly by JPL and Microchip Technology Inc., based in Chandler, Arizona. The company is working with NASA through a commercial partnership, and sample chips have already been shared with defense and commercial aerospace partners.

The technology is expected to play a major role in the future of autonomous spacecraft. With onboard artificial intelligence, spacecraft could respond to unexpected situations in real time when communication delays make human control impractical. The chip could also help deep space missions process, store, and transmit massive amounts of scientific data back to Earth more efficiently.

NASA says the processor may eventually support crewed missions to the Moon and Mars as well.

Small Processor With Massive Computing Power

The device is known as a system-on-a-chip (or SoC), meaning it combines the essential components of a computer into a single compact unit. The processor includes central processing units, computational offloads, advanced networking systems, memory, and input/output interfaces.

SoCs are widely used in smartphones and tablets because they are compact and energy efficient. However, NASA's version is designed to survive for years in deep space, potentially traveling millions (or even billions) of miles from Earth without maintenance or repairs.

Once the processor is certified for use in space, NASA plans to integrate it into a wide variety of missions, including Earth orbiters, planetary rovers, deep space probes, and crewed habitats.

The technology could also have benefits on Earth. Microchip plans to adapt the processor for industries such as aviation and automotive manufacturing.

The project is managed by the Space Technology Mission Directorate's Game Changing Development (GCD) program at NASA Langley. The GCD program and JPL, which is managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, oversaw the development process from mission planning and industry studies through final delivery.

NASA JPL selected Microchip as a partner in 2022, and the company funded its own research and development work on the processor.

Materials provided by NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10680. Generators and Iterators in JavaScript: Lazy Data and Async Flows

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most JavaScript code loads data first and processes it afterward. That works for a list of ten items, but it falls apart when the list has a million entries, never ends, or arrives one network page at a time. Generators and iterators offer another approach: produce values only when someone asks for them. This article explains the iteration protocol, shows how generators build on it, and finishes with async generators for paginated data.

An iterator is any object with a next() method. Each call returns an object with two properties: value, the next item, and done, a boolean that becomes true when the sequence ends. An iterable is an object that hands out an iterator through a method keyed by Symbol.iterator. Arrays, strings, Maps and Sets are all iterables, which is why for...of, spread syntax and destructuring work on them.

Spread syntax calls [Symbol.iterator]() once, then calls next() until done is true. The code is correct but noisy. You manage the state yourself, and every new sequence needs the same scaffolding.

A generator function, declared with function*, returns an iterator without the manual bookkeeping. The yield keyword pauses the function and sends a value to the caller. The next call to next() resumes execution exactly where it stopped, with all local variables intact.

Calling countdown(3) does not run the body. It creates a generator object, and the body runs in slices as the consumer asks for values. Generator objects are iterable too, so spread and for...of work on them directly. The suspended state is the whole trick: the function body acts as a small state machine that the engine writes for you.

Values can also flow back in. The argument passed to next() becomes the result of the yield expression:

The first call to next() only runs the generator up to its first yield, so any argument passed to it is ignored. That detail trips up many developers.

Laziness is where generators earn their place. Array methods such as map and filter process the whole array at each step and allocate a new array each time. A generator pipeline handles one item at a time through the entire chain, which makes it possible to work with sequences that have no end:

The naturals generator never finishes, yet the pipeline completes because take stops asking after four results. No array of squares is ever built, and no number beyond 49 is computed.

There is a useful cleanup rule here. When a for...of loop exits early, through break, return or a thrown error, it calls the iterator's return() method. For a generator, that runs any finally blocks and ends the sequence. In the pipeline above, finishing take releases the generators upstream as well, so a generator that holds a file handle or a database cursor can close it inside a finally block.

Real applications spend more time waiting than computing, and async generators bring the same lazy model to asynchronous work. Declare one with async function* and consume it with for await...of. Each iteration waits for a promise to settle before the loop body runs.

A paginated API is a natural fit. Assume each response contains an items array and a nextUrl property that is null on the last page. This generator requests one page at a time and yields its items:

Once collectTitles has enough titles, the return statement ends the loop, which calls return() on the async generator. No further requests are made. Without a generator you would write a recursive function or a manual loop with its own stopping logic, and callers would have no say over the pace.

Async iteration also suits streams. In Node.js, readable streams are async iterable, so for await...of lets you process a large file chunk by chunk instead of holding all of it in memory.

Iterators define how to walk a sequence, generators make writing them painless, and async generators extend the same idea to data that arrives over time. Start small: replace one function that builds a large intermediate array with a generator, or one manual pagination loop with an async generator. The pull-based model keeps memory use predictable and gives the consumer control over how much work gets done.
