#10678. Japanese pro wrestler allowed to wear mask in city council after winning election

A Japanese professional wrestler who was elected to his local city council has been given permission to wear his signature mask during official meetings, but there’s one condition.

Billyken Kid, a 52-year-old wrestler with Osaka Pro Wrestling, was elected to the Kawanishi City Council in Japan’s Hyogo Prefecture on October 18.

The masked wrestler didn’t just scrape into office, either. He received 4,040 votes, finishing first among 37 candidates competing for 24 seats.

However, his election raised an unusual question for fellow council members: Would he be allowed to wear his wrestling mask while conducting official government business?

According to The Mainichi, the issue sparked discussions among council representatives before they ultimately agreed to let him keep his trademark appearance.

On November 6, representatives from the council’s five political groups voted on whether Billyken Kid could wear his mask inside the chamber.

Four groups supported the request, while one opposed it, raising concerns about why he needed to wear a mask when he had previously attended other meetings without one.

The council eventually approved his request, provided he allows officials to verify his identity by showing his face before entering the chamber.

He must also wear the same mask featured on his election posters and maintain proper conduct during proceedings.

As reported by MBS News, Billyken Kid attended his first council meeting on November 9 wearing a suit and his signature gold wrestling mask.

“Putting on the mask gets me fired up. It also makes me feel more focused,” he explained.

The wrestler added that he hopes to help resolve residents’ problems during his time in office.

While he can wear the mask during council meetings, Billyken Kid says he will remove it for certain ceremonies, including memorial services and funerals.

#10677. Forget electrons, this breakthrough uses light-matter particles to power AI

Eighty years after the creation of ENIAC, the world's first general-purpose electronic computer, researchers at the University of Pennsylvania are exploring a new way to power the future of computing. Instead of relying entirely on electrons, which have formed the backbone of computers since the 1940s, scientists are now turning to light.

ENIAC, developed by Penn researchers J. Presper Eckert and John Mauchly, helped launch the modern computing era by using streams of electrons to solve complex mathematical problems. That same electronic approach still powers today's computers, smartphones, and AI systems. But as artificial intelligence grows more demanding, the limits of electron-based hardware are becoming harder to ignore.

Electrons carry an electrical charge, which creates several challenges inside modern computer chips. As they move through materials, they generate heat and face resistance that wastes energy. Those problems become even more difficult as chips grow more complex and process enormous amounts of data for AI applications.

Researchers led by Penn physicist Bo Zhen in the School of Arts & Sciences believe photons, the particles that make up light, could help solve some of these issues.

"Because they are charge-neutral and have zero rest mass, photons can carry information quickly over long distances with minimal loss, dominating communications technology," explains Li He, co-first author of a paper published in Physical Review Letters and a former postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab. "But that neutrality means they barely interact with their environment, making them bad at the sort of signal-switching logic that computers depend on."

In other words, light is excellent for carrying information quickly and efficiently, but it struggles with the switching operations needed for computing.

Combining Light and Matter for AI Computing

To overcome that problem, Zhen's team developed a special quasiparticle called an exciton-polariton. The particle forms when photons are strongly linked with electrons inside an atomically thin semiconductor material. This combination allows light to interact much more effectively, making it capable of performing the signal switching required for computing tasks.

The breakthrough could be especially important for artificial intelligence systems, which consume enormous amounts of power.

Many experimental photonic AI chips already use light to handle certain calculations at high speed. However, when these systems need to perform nonlinear activation steps, such as decision-making operations, they usually must convert light signals back into electronic ones. That conversion slows the process and increases energy use, reducing the benefits of photonic computing.

Using exciton-polaritons, the Penn researchers demonstrated all-light switching while using only about 4 quadrillionths of a joule of energy. That amount is extraordinarily small, far below the energy needed to briefly power a tiny LED light.

Toward Faster and More Efficient AI Chips

If the technology can be successfully scaled, it could lead to photonic chips capable of processing information directly from cameras without repeated conversions between light and electricity. The approach could also lower the massive energy demands of large AI systems and potentially support basic quantum computing functions on future chips.

Bo Zhen is the Jin K. Lee Presidential Associate Professor in the Department of Physics and Astronomy in the School of Arts & Sciences at the University of Pennsylvania.

Li He was a postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab in Penn Arts & Sciences. He is currently an assistant professor at Montana State University.

Additional authors on the study include Zhi Wang and Bumho Kim from the University of Pennsylvania's School of Arts & Sciences.

The research was supported by the US Office of Naval Research (N00014-20-1-2325 and N00014-21-1-2703) and the Sloan Foundation.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has admitted to having an affair with a woman who is allegedly a Chinese spy. His apology came in a letter to Nvidia staff, which you can read in full below. Nvidia's board of directors released a statement late on Sunday to address the bombshell report that U.S. intelligence believes Huang had an affair with a Chinese spy. The board says it has no reason to believe sensitive corporate information or U.S. national security was compromised, but the company intends to examine all available information to determine what happened.

The BoD claims it is fully confident in Jensen Huang and his leadership team. Huang, who has been married to his wife, Lori Huang, for 41 years, says he fully supports the investigation.

"A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside independent counsel, will oversee the investigation," a statement by Nvidia's BoD reads. "The Board prioritizes U.S. national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders. […] Jensen fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it."

On Friday, the first reporting emerged claiming U.S. intelligence agencies believe Huang had a romantic relationship with Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed who was allegedly recruited by China's Ministry of State Security (MSS) to gather information and help Chinese companies obtain restricted AI GPUs. The company has been on the radar for smuggling restricted hardware to China since at least 2025: Megaspeed has been accused of smuggling $2 billion worth of AI chips into China via its subsidiary Speedmatrix.

To my colleagues at NVIDIA, I owe my family and all of you an apology.

Nvidia has strenuously rejected suggestions of improper dealings, saying that its CEO regularly meets thousands of customers and that these interactions do not involve sensitive corporate information or national security secrets. Chinese officials have also dismissed the espionage allegations as unfounded.

The suspected Chinese intelligence operation targeting Jensen has not been independently confirmed as a fact. The relationship is alleged to follow the typical "honeypot" strategy, wherein foreign intelligence agencies use operatives to ensnare targets into romantic relationships to steal information and influence their decision-making.

Huang has been an outspoken critic of those opposed to limiting the supply of AI GPUs to China. Huang recently expressed his opinion that the label 'China hawk' is not a badge of honor Americans should wear; it is really 'a badge of shame.' Huang also said it "doesn’t have to be all us or them. It could be us and them," drawing fire from others in the administration and other tech companies, including the CEO of Palantir.

#10673. Amazon unveils $250 security drone that patrols your house and flies between rooms

Amazon has unveiled a $250 AI-powered security camera that can fly around your house on its own to investigate suspicious activity.

Announced on November 10, the Ring Drone Cam can travel between rooms, check doors and windows, and automatically respond when a connected security camera, sensor, or alarm is triggered.

Unlike traditional security cameras, which remain fixed in one location, the Ring Drone Cam uses AI to navigate around furniture and other obstacles.

Owners can send it to specific locations, set custom flight routes, or schedule regular patrols around their homes.

If a connected security device detects activity, the drone can automatically fly to a predetermined location to investigate, allowing owners to check what’s happening remotely.

Amazon says the camera records in 2K resolution and features built-in lights for capturing footage in darker rooms. When not in use, it returns to its charging dock, where its camera remains switched off.

The company has also announced the Ring Rover Cam, a $999.99 wheeled security robot designed for businesses.

Rather than flying, the Rover patrols stores and offices, using an adjustable camera to see over counters and shelving. It can also investigate triggered alarms and activate a built-in siren and light tower.

The Rover is scheduled to begin shipping in the US on December 16, 2026, while the $249.99 Drone Cam is targeting January 27, 2027.

However, the flying camera still requires authorization from the Federal Communications Commission before it can be sold. Certain features, including scheduled patrols, also require a compatible Ring Protect subscription.

#10672. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Colin Klein and Andrew Barron. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10669. How to Source Passive Candidates with AI in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Passive candidates answer when a message is specific, short and clearly about them. This is a nine-step playbook, and each step shows how to do it in Pin. The method works in any tool. For a shorter version, see Pin's 7-step guide to sourcing passive candidates.

For recruiters sourcing passive talent at scale, Pin covers the workflow end to end. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and ranks them against your brief, and the recruiter makes every call.

A passive candidate is a working professional who isn't applying for jobs but might say yes to the right conversation.

Gallup's fourth-quarter 2025 panel sizes the pool. It found that 51% of U.S. workers are actively looking (11%) or watching for opportunities (40%).

The practical reading: only 11% of workers are actively looking, so most of the market isn't on a job board, and about half is paying attention.

Write the brief as outcomes and skills, not a title. Use five lines:

Skills-based pools are larger. LinkedIn's Economic Graph estimated in March 2025 that defining eligibility by skills rather than prior job title would expand the typical talent pool by a median 6.1x globally and 15.9x in the United States. That is a modeled estimate, with "eligible" meaning at least half of the target job's top skills.

In Pin: paste the job description, add a job listing link, upload two or three ideal résumés, or import the role from your ATS. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Answer them. They are the parts of the brief you hadn't written down.

Pin says its AI sourcing searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, aggregated from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Ryan Levy, Managing Partner at Cruit Group, says: "Pin gave us the ability to find candidates that didn't appear on LinkedIn Recruiter. The platform is easy to use and is continuing to evolve!"

Here is the same search written two ways. Pin's Boolean search guide for recruiters covers the operators in the first version.

("senior data engineer" OR "staff data engineer" OR "lead data engineer") AND (Snowflake OR BigQuery OR Redshift) AND (dbt OR Airflow) AND (fintech OR payments OR "financial services") NOT (intern OR junior OR consultant OR recruiter)

Senior data engineer who has built a warehouse from scratch at a Series B to D fintech. Strong dbt and Airflow. Remote in US time zones. Avoid consultancies.

The string misses anyone titled "Software Engineer, Data Platform" who has done the same work. Skills-based matching reads the work, so title mismatches stop hiding good people. Filters can be written in natural language, and advanced filters stay under your control.

Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, puts it this way: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

A ranked list is only useful if you can see why each person ranks. Pin gives every candidate a fit score and an AI-written reason. Read the reason first, then accept or reject.

Pin learns from accept and reject feedback across the team, so the second batch should look better than the first. If the reasons show the wrong kind of match, fix the brief rather than rejecting one person at a time.

Spend contact credits after the shortlist, not before it. Pin returns personal email, work email and phone number, at 1 credit each. The Free plan includes 5 credits a week. Solo includes 500 a month, and Professional includes 1,000 per seat a month.

Reference the person's actual work, give one reason this role might fit, and make one small ask. Keep it short. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's own profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice, so you edit rather than start blank. Email steps send automatically. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Pin's guide to engaging passive candidates goes deeper on message length and personalization.

The messages below are illustrative. They are templates with placeholders, not real results.

Hi [First name], I read your write-up on moving [Company]'s billing pipeline to dbt. The point about [specific lesson] is something we're working through too.

I'm hiring a senior data engineer at [Company]. The job is to [one-sentence outcome]. It's remote in US time zones.

Worth a 15-minute call to see if it's relevant? If not, no problem at all.

Message 2: follow-up in the same thread (about day 3)

Hi [First name], one detail I left out: [team size, comp range or what the first 90 days look like]. If the timing is wrong, tell me and I'll check back in a few months.

Message 3: an SMS task you send (about day 5, only where you have a number and the rules allow)

Hi [First name], [Your name] from [Company]. I emailed about a senior data engineer role. Worth a quick chat? Happy to stop here if not.

Pin reports that its multi-channel outreach sequences deliver 5x better response rates than industry averages, measured against LinkedIn's published 10-25% InMail reply benchmark. That figure is vendor-reported, so test it against your own baseline.

Follow-ups do most of the work. In Pin's Recruiting Outreach Benchmarks 2026, covering 4,000,000+ messages sent between June 2025 and May 2026, follow-ups earned 56.2% of all replies. The first three touches captured 93.2% of the replies a sequence earns, and a fourth raised that to 97.7%. Half of replies arrived within 4 hours and 74.8% within a day, so spacing touches about two business days apart gives each message its chance. The data comes from Pin's own platform, so treat it as a starting point.

A workable plan: three to four touches about two business days apart. Change the content each time, and add a channel when you have a legitimate way to reach the person. In the same data, sequences that paired email with a LinkedIn message were associated with 2 to 4x the replies of email alone at the same touch count. Recruiters add the LinkedIn touch for the candidates they want most, so part of that gap reflects prioritization.

Pin's multi-channel sequences combine automated email steps with AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Email sends on schedule. Pin queues each LinkedIn and SMS step as a task with the message drafted, and you send it. Sequences adapt per candidate, any reply pauses the sequence, and every channel for a candidate stays in one thread. A shared candidate pool with a dedupe layer keeps two recruiters from contacting the same person. The Free plan has email-only sequences. Multi-channel sequences start on Solo.

Passive candidates book around a day job, so offer early, late and lunchtime slots. Pin's AI interview scheduling handles time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, and confirmations land in the ATS.

Track positive replies separately, since a reply is not the same as interest.

Change one variable at a time, such as the opening line or the day of the follow-up, and compare within the same role. Pin's funnel dashboards show pipeline velocity, conversion by stage and activity by recruiter. Pin's 2026 user survey across 2,000+ organizations and 20,000+ users reports a 14-day average time-to-fill and 12 hours saved per recruiter per week. Test those vendor-reported figures against your own.

Once steps 1 to 8 work by hand, hand over the repetitive ones. Pin's AI recruiting agent has two modes. In Assist, you approve each step. In Autopilot, the agent sources, screens and ranks, drafts outreach, sends the email steps, follows up on silence, schedules interviews and updates the ATS. LinkedIn and SMS steps stay tasks that you send. Closing and offers stay human. For how other agents compare, see best AI recruiting agents.

Start in Assist on your first two roles and read every draft. Move email follow-ups and scheduling to Autopilot first, since they repeat most. Guardrails keep a human in the loop, every action appears in a feed, and the agent runs 24/7.

Pin also has an MCP server. It lets AI assistants such as Claude Desktop or ChatGPT set up searches, review candidates and tune ranking, and its page says outreach can't be sent silently.

Miles Randle, Head of People & Talent at Flip CX, says: "As a small people and talent team, we don't have a ton of time to spend hours sourcing and messaging. Pin has made it possible for us to focus on the people side of things!"

#10668. Xfinity offers free GTA 6 speed boost to download the massive 212GB game in 30 minutes

Xfinity handed its internet customers a free 15-day speed boost of up to 2 Gbps, so they could download GTA 6 faster.

The offer landed just as Rockstar flung open digital pre-loads on November 10, confirming the hotly anticipated game would need up to 212GB, or roughly a third of a base PS5’s usable storage.

With a download that size looming, Spectrum had already promised its customers a free gig upgrade, and it didn’t take long for Xfinity to follow.

Comcast’s November 9 announcement confirms eligible Xfinity Internet members can activate the boost through the Xfinity app or the Membership page at Xfinity.com/membership.

Once claimed, it lifts download speeds to up to 2 gigabits per second for 15 days, which Comcast says is enough to download GTA 6 in as little as 30 minutes.

The redemption window stays open until December 3, so anyone who claims it on the final day keeps the faster speeds until December 18. Meanwhile, members who don’t see the option on their Membership page can contact Xfinity Customer Service to request access.

Comcast pitched the boost as a way to beat the rush. Kohposh Kuda, Senior Vice President of Consumer Product Marketing, said millions of people would “hit download the moment Grand Theft Auto VI becomes available.” In fact, Xfinity expected the stretch from November 10 to 19 to be the busiest week ever on its network, with players barreling toward pre-loads well before launch.

The company also pointed members toward its year-round Boost a Device feature, which prioritizes the WiFi connection for up to three devices, such as a console, on a home network.

#10667. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

#10666. McDonald’s new chicken sandwiches are so popular they’re being pulled from menus

McDonald’s Japan is temporarily pulling several chicken sandwiches from its menu after demand for its new lineup exceeded expectations, leaving the fast food chain struggling to maintain supplies.

The company announced on November 10 that sales of its Zakuzaku Juicy Mac lineup will be suspended nationwide beginning November 13.

The decision comes after the sandwiches proved more popular than anticipated, creating shortages of the chicken patties used to make them.

The suspension affects several permanent menu items, while a separate limited-time chicken sandwich will disappear earlier than originally planned.

In its announcement, McDonald’s Japan explained that stronger-than-expected sales have made it difficult to consistently secure enough chicken patties for its new menu items.

The company confirmed that seven products will be temporarily removed from menus across Japan, including three standard sandwiches, three double-patty versions available through its Night Mac menu, and a chicken side dish.

McDonald’s also confirmed that its limited-time Cheese Cheese Teriyaki Chicken Juicy Mac will end its run earlier than originally scheduled.

The company warned that individual restaurants could run out of affected products before November 13, depending on their remaining inventory.

Despite the shortages, other chicken products will remain available, including Chicken McNuggets, the McChicken, and the Chicken McMuffin. Several other Night Mac offerings will also continue to be sold as usual.

McDonald’s expects to resume sales of the suspended permanent menu items by the end of November, although an exact return date has not been announced.

McDonald’s reveals wild new sandwich that doesn’t use a bun

The company said it will provide further updates through its official website once preparations to resume sales are complete.

McDonald’s Japan apologized to customers affected by the temporary suspension and thanked them for their understanding as it works to restore supplies.

The temporary suspension comes as McDonald’s Japan prepares to launch a new Genshin Impact collaboration on November 18, featuring special packaging inspired by characters Varesa and Escoffier.

The promotion, which runs through December 8, will feature themed packaging for the Teriyaki Mac Burger and Double Teriyaki Mac Burger, alongside exclusive in-game rewards that customers can redeem through the My McDonald’s Rewards program.
